Holomorphic curves in the 6 6 -pseudosphere and cyclic surfaces
Brian Collier, Jérémy Toulisse

Abstract
. The space 𝐇 4 , 2 \mathbf{H}^{4,2} of vectors of norm − 1 -1 in ℝ 4 , 3 \mathbb{R}^{4,3} has a natural pseudo-Riemannian metric and a compatible almost complex structure. The group of automorphisms of both of these structures is the split real form 𝖦 2 ′ \mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime} . In this paper we consider a class of holomorphic curves in 𝐇 4 , 2 \mathbf{H}^{4,2} which we call alternating. We show that such curves admit a so called Frenet framing. Using this framing, we show that the space of alternating holomorphic curves which are equivariant with respect to a surface group are naturally parameterized by certain 𝖦 2 ′ \mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime} -Higgs bundles. This leads to a holomorphic description of the moduli space as a fibration over Teichmüller space with a holomorphic action of the mapping class group. Using a generalization of Labourie’s cyclic surfaces, we then show that equivariant alternating holomorphic curves are infinitesimally rigid.



1.
Introduction

The pseudohyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is the set of vectors of norm
-1
in a real vector space equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form of signature
(4,3)
. There is a natural signature
(4,2)
pseudo-Riemannian metric on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Multiplication in the split octonion algebra defines a nonintegrable almost complex structure on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which is compatible with the pseudo-Riemannian metric.
This is the noncompact analogue of the famous almost complex structure on the
6
-sphere.
The group which preserves this structure is the split real Lie group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
of type
G_{2}.

In this paper, we consider a class of
\mathbf{J}
-holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which we call
alternating
. Similar to parameterized curves in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
generically admit a Frenet framing.
The alternating condition concerns the signature of the subbundles in the Frenet framing, and can be equivalently described using cyclic Higgs bundles. We focus especially on alternating holomorphic curves which are preserved by a surface group, and describe the resulting moduli space.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 2
. For a real semisimple Lie group
\mathsf{G}
, the character variety
\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G})
is the space of
\mathsf{G}
-conjugacy classes of reductive group homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}
from the fundamental group of
\Sigma
into
\mathsf{G}
.
For some Lie groups, the character variety has distinguished connected components which generalize many features of the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
. Such components will be called higher rank Teichmüller spaces. These spaces have been studied in detail by a variety of authors from various perspectives, see for example
[
29
,
35
,
18
,
14
]
.

When the rank of
\mathsf{G}
is two, the relevant Lie groups are locally isomorphic to
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
,
\mathsf{SO}(2,n)
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
; the relevant spaces are known as the Hitchin components for
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
,
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
, and the space of maximal representations for
\mathsf{SO}(2,n)
.
Each of these spaces admits a mapping class group invariant complex analytic parameterization as a fibration over the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
.
This was described independently by Loftin
[
23
]
and Labourie
[
20
]
for
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and Labourie
[
19
]
for Hitchin components; for maximal representations it was described by Alessandrini and the first author
[
2
]
and Tholozan and the authors
[
7
]
.

The complex analytic description has two inputs, a parameterization of the spaces using Higgs bundles on a fixed Riemann surface and the uniqueness of a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant minimal surface in the Riemannian symmetric space of the group.
A central tool in the rank 2 analysis is the restriction to a set of Higgs bundles which have a cyclic group symmetry.
When the rank of the group is at least 3, the relevant Higgs bundles do not necessarily have a cyclic symmetry. In fact, Markovic, Sagman and Smillie recently showed that, in rank at least 3, there are representations in higher rank Teichmüller spaces which have multiple
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant minimal surface in the Riemannian symmetric space
[
24
,
25
,
30
]
.
Among other thing, this breakthrough suggests that representations arising from cyclic Higgs bundles deserve special attention.

For
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
Hitchin representations, the uniqueness of the minimal surface was deduced from the uniqueness of an invariant affine sphere. Similarly, for
\mathsf{SO}(2,n)
maximal representations, the uniqueness of the minimal surface follows from the uniqueness of an invariant maximal spacelike surface in the pseudohyperbolic space
\mathbf{H}^{2,n-1}.
In both cases, these surfaces characterize the appropriate higher rank Teichmüller space. That is, they only exist for representations in the
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component or for maximal
\mathsf{SO}(2,n)
-representations, respectively.
In
[
4
]
, Baraglia pioneered the use of cyclic Higgs bundles to study Hitchin representations in rank 2. In particular, he recovered the affine sphere description for
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and proved that Hitchin representations into
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
define equivariant holomorphic curves
f:\widetilde{X}\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}.

The equivariant holomorphic curves constructed by Baraglia satisfy the alternating condition defined in this paper. However, we prove that preserving an alternating holomorphic curve in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
does not characterize the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
Hitchin component.
As a result, the theory of alternating holomorphic curves identifies representations
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
which are not Hitchin representations but share some features with Hitchin representations.
This is similar to what happens for
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
. Namely,
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
Hitchin representations are maximal, and so preserve a maximal spacelike surface in
\mathbf{H}^{2,2}
, but this does not characterize
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
Hitchin representations since there are non-Hitchin maximal
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
representations.
Unlike the
\mathsf{SO}(2,3)
situation, the only higher rank Teichmüller space for the group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is the Hitchin component.
In particular, little is known about the non-Hitchin
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
representations which preserve an alternating holomorphic curve. It would be interesting to determine whether these
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
representations are Anosov, and to study the uniqueness properties of the alternating holomorphic curves.

1.1.
Alternating holomorphic curves

Let
S
be an oriented surface, and
(\mathbf{H}^{4,2},\mathbf{J})
be the almost complex manifold described above. An immersion
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is called a holomorphic curve if the image of the tangent bundle is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant. This induces a Riemann surface structure on
S.

As mentioned above, holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
generically admit a Frenet framing. We say that
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is
alternating
if the tangent bundle of
S
is positive definite and the image of the second fundamental form is negative definite. It turns out that when the second fundamental form is nonzero, its image defines a subbundle of rank two, see §
3.4
for details.
Alternating holomorphic curves are in particular maximal spacelike surfaces.

By construction, the Frenet framing defines a lift to a homogeneous space
\mathbf{X}
which we call the cyclic space. Surfaces in
\mathbf{X}
arising from the Frenet lift of alternating holomorphic curves are very particular and define what we call
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces
, a slight generalization of the cyclic surfaces introduced by Labourie in
[
19
]
.
A holomorphic interpretation of the Frenet framing defines a special type of cyclic
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
harmonic bundles on the Riemann surface induced by
f
. That is, cyclic
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles equipped with a cyclic harmonic metric, see §
4.5
.

To establish a 1-1 correspondence between isomorphism classes of these objects, it is necessary to consider equivariant alternating holomorphic curves. Such objects are pairs
(\rho,f)
, where
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is a representation and
f:\widetilde{S}\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is a alternating holomorphic curve which is
\rho
-equivariant. There are natural notions of isomorphisms in this setting.

Theorem A
.

Let
S
be an oriented surface. Then the Frenet lift defines a one-to-one correspondence between isomorphism classes of

(1)

equivariant alternating holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
,

(2)

equivariant
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces in
\mathbf{X}
, and

(3)

\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
cyclic harmonic bundles of the form (
24
).

Remark 1.1
.

Note that Theorem
A
does not assume the surface
S
is compact. This is why we have
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic bundles rather than polystable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles. In particular, the induced Riemann surfaces could be biholomorphic to the complex plane. Existence of cyclic harmonic metrics has recently been studied by Li and Mochizuki
[
22
,
21
]
.

Now consider a closed oriented surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
. The set of equivalence classes of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves
(\rho,f)
is denoted by

\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)=\{(\rho,f)~\text{equivariant alternating holomorphic curves}\}/\sim~,

where the equivalence is given by the natural action of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}\times\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma).
The mapping class group
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
naturally acts on
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma).
Furthermore, forgetting the map
f
, and taking the induced Riemann surface structure of
f
define
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
-equivariant maps to the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-character variety and the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Hol}\ \ \ }
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)}

For closed surfaces, isomorphism classes of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles on a fixed Riemann surface
X=(\Sigma,\mathsf{j})
are identified with isomorphism classes of polystable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic Higgs bundles.
In Theorem
5.4
, we parameterize isomorphism classes of polystable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles which arise from equivariant alternating holomorphic curves.
Combining this description with Simpson’s construction of the moduli space of Higgs bundles in families
[
33
]
(see also
[
2
]
) leads to a complex analytic structure on
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
.

Theorem B
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
has the structure of a complex analytic space. With respect to this structure, the mapping class group
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
acts analytically and the projection map
\pi:\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is a surjective analytic map with smooth fibers.
Moreover, the space
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
decomposes as

\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)=\coprod_{d\in\{0,\cdots,6g-6\}}\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)~,

where
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
has complex dimension
d+8g-8
.
The fiber of
\pi:\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
over a Riemann surface
X
is

•

a rank
(2d-g+1)
holomorphic vector bundle over the
(6g-6-d)
-symmetric product of
X
when
g\leq d\leq 6g-6
, and

•

a bundle over a
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-cover of the
2d
-symmetric product of
X
whose fiber is
(\mathbb{C}^{5g-5-d}\setminus\{0\})/\pm\mathrm{Id}
when
0\leq d\leq g-1.

For the extremal values
d=6g-6
and
d=0
, the image of the holonomy map can be classified. When
d=6g-6,
the space
\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is a holomorphic vector bundle whose fiber over a Riemann surface
X
are holomorphic differentials of degree
6.

In this case, the holonomy map establishes a diffeomorphism with the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin component. When
d=0,
the space
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma)
has
2^{2g}
connected components, and the image of the holonomy map consists of all representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
which factor through the nonsplit
\mathbb{Z}_{2}

-extension of
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and whose
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-factor is a Hitchin representation, see §
5.5
for more details.
For
0<d<6g-6
, the representations in the image of the holonomy map have not been studied previously.

Holomorphic curves in the almost complex 6-sphere were first studied by Bryant in
[
5
]
. His work can be thought of as an analogue of our Higgs bundle description of alternating holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, where the main difference is that we work in the equivariant case and noncompact symmetric spaces instead of with compact Riemann surfaces and compact symmetric spaces.
In
[
12
]
, Eschenburg and Vlachos prove that a holomorphic curve in
\mathbf{S}^{6}
is contained in
\mathbf{S}^{2}
(the totally geodesic case), a totally geodesic hypersurface
\mathbf{S}^{5}\subset\mathbf{S}^{6}
, or spans the entire sphere (generic case).
Equivariant alternating holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
witness the same phenomenon. They either span
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
or are in a totally geodesic
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the latter case only occurs in
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma).
Furthermore, for
0\leq d\leq g-1,
the components
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
contain natural degenerations of alternating holomorphic curves which lie in totally geodesic copies of the hyperbolic disc.

Finally, we study the infinitesimal properties of the holonomy map
\mathrm{Hol}:\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
. Specifically, we show that alternating holomorphic curves are infinitesimally rigid.

Theorem C
.

For
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, let
(\rho_{t},f_{t})
be a smooth path of equivariant alternating curves such that
[\overset{\bullet}{\rho_{0}}]=0
in the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{[\rho_{0}]}\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
. Then, at
t=0,

(\rho_{t},f_{t})
is tangent to the
\big(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}\times\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)\big)
-orbit through
(\rho_{0},f_{0}).

To prove Theorem
C
, we show that Labourie’s proof of infinitesimal rigidity of the cyclic surfaces arising from Hitchin representations into split rank 2 Lie groups can be adapted to our more general setting. In the process, we streamline many of the main ideas and computations of
[
19
]
. A general theory of cyclic surfaces and infinitesimal rigidity will appear in
[
8
]
.
Theorem
C
should be thought of as saying the holonomy map is an immersion. In particular, for a representation
\rho
in the image of
\mathrm{Hol}
,
\rho
-equivariant alternating holomorphic curves do not come in one parameter families.
In the case of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representations, infinitesimal rigidity is enough to prove global uniqueness of the equivariant alternating holomorphic curve, this is not the case for the components
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
when
0<d<6g-6
.

1.2.
Related results

While this paper was being written, some analogous results were proved. In
[
27
]
, Nie studies equivariant alternating surfaces in
\mathbf{H}^{p,q}
for
(p,q)=(2k,2k)
or
(2k,2k-2)
. The name alternating in this paper was chosen because of Nie’s work. Using different techniques, he proves an infinitesimal rigidity result analogous to Theorem
C
under some assumptions.
For
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, Nie’s results apply to a subset
\mathsf{SO}(4,3)
representations. Interestingly, the intersection of this subset with
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-representations is exactly the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representations. In particular, the overlap of the Nie’s results with Theorem
C
is exactly the case originally covered by Labourie
[
19
]
.

In
[
13
]
, Evans studies holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
when the underlying surface is biholomorphic to the complex plane. The holomorphic curves he considers are alternating and, in many aspects, analogous to those equivariant for a Hitchin representation. It would be interesting if similar analysis applies to general alternating holomorphic curves from the complex plane.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

In §
2
, we recall different constructions of the split octonions and define the group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
. The almost-complex structure on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the notion of alternating holomorphic curves and their Frenet framings are introduced in §
3
. In §
4
we recall the theory of
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle and
\mathsf{G}
-harmonic bundle, then specifying to the case of
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and prove the equivalence between equivariant holomorphic bundles and certain
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
cyclic harmonic bundles. In §
5
, contains the parameterization of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
and properties of the extremal cases.
In §
6
, we develop the notion of cyclic surfaces and prove Theorem
C
.

1.4.
Acknowledgements:

We thank Sorin Dumitrescu, Parker Evans, François Labourie, Xin Nie, Andy Sanders, Carlos Simpson and Richard Wentworth for insightful conversations concerning various aspects of the paper. The first authors research is partially funded by NSF DMS grant 2103685.

2.
Split octonions and the Lie group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}

In this section we discuss the algebra of split octonions and some of its properties. We refer the reader to
[
3
,
11
]
for more details. We start with the quaternions and split quaternions.

2.1.
Quaternions and split quaternions

Recall that the quaternions are the real associative algebra
\mathbb{H}
generated as a
\mathbb{R}
-vector space by
\{1,j,d,e\}
with the relations
1
1

1

We do not use
i,j,k
since we will need
\mathrm{i}
for complexified objects. Our convention is that Greek letters square to
+1
and Roman letters square to
-1.

\textstyle{j^{2}=d^{2}=e^{2}=-1}
and
\textstyle{j\cdot d=-d\cdot j=e~.}

On the other hand, the split quaternions are the real associative algebra
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
generated as a
\mathbb{R}
-vector space by
\{1,j,\delta,\epsilon\}
with the relations

\textstyle{j^{2}=-\delta^{2}=-\epsilon^{2}=-1}
and
\textstyle{j\cdot\delta=-\delta\cdot j=\epsilon~.}

We have a vector space decomposition

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.56947pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-30.56947pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\Im(\mathbb{H})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.56947pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 100.68062pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\mathbb{R}
is the span of the unit
1
and
\Im
is the span of the remaining generators. These spaces are the
\pm 1
-eigenspaces of the conjugation involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
. The projection onto the eigenspaces is given by taking real and imaginary
x=\Re(x)+\Im(x)
, where

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 36.86806pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-36.86806pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Re(x)=\frac{1}{2}(x+\overline{x})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 60.86806pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 106.9792pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Im(x)=\frac{1}{2}(x-\overline{x})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

On the imaginary spaces
\Im(\mathbb{H})
and
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
, taking real and imaginary parts of the product define nondegenerate symmetric bilinear forms
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
with respective signatures
(0,3)
and
(2,1)
, and a skew symmetric products
\times
. Namely, for
x_{1},x_{2}
in
\Im(\mathbb{H})
or
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 44.79166pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-44.79166pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle=\Re(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 68.79166pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 114.9028pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{x_{1}\times x_{2}=\Im(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Combining these structures defines volume forms
\Omega
on
\Im(\mathbb{H})
and
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
defined by

\Omega(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\langle x_{1}\times x_{2},x_{3}\rangle.

In both cases, the group of algebra automorphisms acts trivially on the real part, and so act on imaginary subspace. Hence, the groups
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H})
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
act on respectively
\Im(\mathbb{H})
and
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
preserving
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
,
\times
and
\Omega.
Thus,

\textstyle{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H})<\mathsf{SO}(3)}
and
\textstyle{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})<\mathsf{SO}(2,1)~.}

In fact,
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H})=\mathsf{SO}(3)
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
is the identity component
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(2,1)
of
\mathsf{SO}(2,1).

2.2.
The split octonions from split quaternions

The split octonions
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
can be described by the Cayley-Dickson process on
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
or
\mathbb{H}
. Both perspectives will be useful but we start with the split quaternion description.

Definition 2.1
.

The split octonions
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
are the real algebra with underlying vector space
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
equipped with the product

(1)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 26.37505pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.37505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 50.37505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{with}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 98.51936pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(x_{1},y_{2})\cdot(x_{2},y_{2})=(x_{1}\cdot x_{2}-\overline{y}_{2}\cdot y_{1}~,~y_{2}\cdot x_{1}+y_{1}\cdot\overline{x}_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

The group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is defined to be the group of algebra automorphisms of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
. It is the split real form of the exceptional complex simple Lie group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

We will write
\ell=(0,1)\in\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, and write a split octonion
z=(x,y)
as
z=x+y\ell
. Note that
\ell^{2}=-1
and that, for any
y\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
, we have

y\ell=y\cdot\ell=(y,0)\cdot(0,1)=(0,y)=-\ell\cdot y~.

Nonassociativity of the product can be seen by considering
(j\cdot\delta)\cdot\ell
and
j\cdot(\delta\cdot\ell)
since

\textstyle{(j\cdot\delta)\cdot\ell=(\epsilon,0)\cdot(0,1)=\epsilon\ell}
and
\textstyle{j\cdot(\delta\cdot\ell)=(j,0)\cdot(0,\delta)=(0,-\epsilon)=-\epsilon\ell~.}

However, one checks that any subalgebra generated by two elements of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is associative. In particular, for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
we have

(2)

z_{1}\cdot(z_{1}\cdot z_{2})=(z_{1}\cdot z_{1})\cdot z_{2}~.

We have a decomposition
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
, where
\mathbb{R}
is the span of the unit
1
and
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
. These are the
\pm 1
-eigenspaces of the conjugation involution

\textstyle{z=x+y\ell\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{z}=\overline{x}-y\ell~.}

Since any two distinct generators
\{j,\delta,\epsilon,\ell,j\ell,\delta\ell,\epsilon\ell\}
of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
anticommute, for all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
we have

\overline{z_{1}\cdot z_{2}}=\overline{z}_{2}\cdot\overline{z}_{1}=z_{2}\cdot z_{1}~.

There is a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form and a skew symmetric product defined by

\textstyle{\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=\Re(z_{1}\cdot z_{2})=\frac{1}{2}(z_{1}\cdot z_{2}+\overline{z_{1}\cdot z_{2}})}
and
\textstyle{z_{1}\times z_{2}=\Im(z_{1}\cdot z_{2})=\frac{1}{2}(z_{1}\cdot z_{2}-\overline{z_{1}\cdot z_{2}})~.}

Let
\mathbf{q}
be the quadratic form on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
associated to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle.
Some immediate consequences of these definitions of
(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q},\times)
are:

Lemma 2.2
.

•

The signature of
\mathbf{q}
is
(4,3)
and
\{j,\delta,\epsilon,\ell,j\ell,\delta\ell,\epsilon\ell\}
is an orthonormal basis.

•

z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
are orthogonal if and only if
z_{1}\times z_{2}=z_{1}\cdot z_{2}.

•

\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is an orthogonal splitting, and the restriction of
\mathbf{q}
to
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
and
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
has signature
(2,1)
and
(2,2)
, respectively.

•

For
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
and
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
, we have

\textstyle{z_{1}\times z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{z_{1}\times w_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}}
and
\textstyle{w_{1}\times w_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})~.}

•

There is a three from
\Omega\in\Lambda^{3}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})^{*})
defined by

(3)

\Omega(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\langle z_{1}\times z_{2},z_{3}\rangle~.

Remark 2.3
.

The algebra structure on
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is fully determined by
(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q},\times)
since for
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
we have

(a_{1}+z_{1})\cdot(a_{2}+z_{2})=a_{1}a_{2}+\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle+a_{2}z_{1}+a_{1}z_{2}+z_{1}\times z_{2}.

The group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
acts trivially on the real part. Hence,
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
acts on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
preserving
\mathbf{q}
,
\times
and
\Omega
. Since the algebraic structure of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is fully encoded in
(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q},\times)
,
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
has the following description.

Proposition 2.4
.

The group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the group of linear transformations of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
which preserve
\mathbf{q}
and
\times.
Such an automorphism also preserves
\Omega.

Derivations of
\times
defines the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}=\{X\in\mathsf{End}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))~|~X(z_{1}\times z_{2})=(X(z_{1})\times z_{2})+(z_{1}\times X(z_{2}))\text{ for all }z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})\}.

In particular,
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}<\mathsf{O}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})\cong\mathsf{O}(4,3)
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{so}(4,3).

It follows from
[
11
, Lemma 4.2]
that there is a unique
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant volume form
\operatorname{vol}\in\Lambda^{7}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})^{*})
such that,

(4)

\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle\operatorname{vol}=\iota_{z_{1}}\Omega\wedge\iota_{z_{2}}\Omega\wedge\Omega

for all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
. Hence
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}<\mathsf{SO}(4,3)
.
Explicitly, in the basis
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{7}\}=\{j,\delta,\epsilon,\ell,j\ell,\delta\ell,\epsilon\ell\}
, we have

\Omega=6\Big[f_{1}^{*}\wedge(f_{2}^{*}\wedge f_{3}^{*}-f_{4}^{*}\wedge f_{5}^{*}-\wedge f_{6}^{*}\wedge f_{7}^{*})+f_{2}^{*}\wedge(f_{4}^{*}\wedge f_{6}^{*}+f_{5}^{*}\wedge f_{7}^{*})+f_{3}^{*}\wedge(f_{4}^{*}\wedge f_{7}^{*}-f_{5}^{*}\wedge f_{6}^{*})\Big]~.

and one checks
\operatorname{vol}=-\frac{1}{144}f_{1}^{*}\wedge f_{2}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge f_{7}^{*}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
which preserves the splitting
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SO}(2,2)
.

Proof.

Let
g=(g_{1}\oplus g_{2})
be an element of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
which preserves the splitting
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
preserve the quadratic form
\mathbf{q}
, then
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathsf{O}(\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})\times\mathsf{O}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}).

Observe that the restriction of the
3
-form
\Omega
from (
3
) to
\Lambda^{3}(\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}))
is nonzero. Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
preserves
\Omega
and the volume form from (
4
), we conclude that
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathsf{SO}(\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})\times\mathsf{SO}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}).

To conclude the proof,
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
preserves the product
\times
and

\textstyle{j=\ell\times j\ell~,}
\textstyle{\delta=\ell\times\delta\ell}
and
\textstyle{\epsilon=\ell\times\epsilon\ell~.}

Hence, any
g_{2}\in\in\mathsf{SO}(\mathbb{H}^{\prime},\mathbf{q})
uniquely determines
g_{1}\in\mathsf{SO}(\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})
.
∎

2.3.
Split octonions from quaternions

The split octonions can also be defined using the quaternions:

(5)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.56949pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.56949pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=\mathbb{H}\oplus\mathbb{H}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.56949pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{with}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 97.01398pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(a_{1},b_{1})\cdot(a_{2},b_{2})=(a_{1}\cdot a_{2}+\overline{b}_{2}\cdot b_{1}~,~b_{2}\cdot a_{1}+b_{1}\cdot\overline{a}_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

We will write
(a,b)=a+b\lambda
, where
\lambda=(0,1)
. Note that
\lambda^{2}=+1.

An isomorphism between these two presentations of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
is given by

(6)

\{1,j,\delta,\epsilon,\ell,j\ell,\delta\ell,\epsilon\ell\}\to\{1,j,e\lambda,d\lambda,d,e,j\lambda,\lambda\}.

In this presentation,
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathbb{H}
, and the restriction of
\mathbf{q}
to the summands
\Im(\mathbb{H})
and
\mathbb{H}
have signatures
(0,3)
and
(4,0)
, respectively.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be the subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
which preserves the splitting
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathbb{H}
. Then we have

\mathsf{K}\cong\mathsf{SO}(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{q})\cong\mathsf{SO}(4).

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

The exterior product decomposes as
\Lambda^{2}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))\cong\Lambda^{2}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))\oplus\Im(\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{H}\oplus\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{H}
. The restriction,
\times
to
\Im(\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{H}
is zero while the restrictions to the other summands give maps

\textstyle{\times|_{\Lambda^{2}\Im(\mathbb{H})}:\Lambda^{2}\Im(\mathbb{H})\to\Im(\mathbb{H})}
and
\textstyle{\times|_{\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{H}}:\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{H}\to\Im(\mathbb{H})~.}

The first map is an isomorphism. The second map defines an isomorphism between the vector space
\Lambda_{+}^{2}\mathbb{H}
of self-dual two forms on
\mathbb{H}
with respect to
\mathbf{q}_{\mathbb{H}}
and the volume form
\lambda\wedge(j\lambda)\wedge(d\lambda)\wedge(e\lambda)
and
\Im(\mathbb{H})
.

2.4.
Complexification of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})

Let
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the complexification of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
and we will denote the complex linear extensions of
\mathbf{q}
and
\times
by the same symbol. Elements of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}
will be written
w+\mathrm{i}z
where
w,z\in\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}).

The following basis
\{f_{-3},f_{-2},\cdots,f_{3}\}
of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}
will be used often:

(7)

\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta\ell+\mathrm{i}\epsilon\ell),~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ell+\mathrm{i}j\ell),~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta+\mathrm{i}\epsilon),~j,~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta-\mathrm{i}\epsilon),~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ell-\mathrm{i}j\ell),~\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta\ell-\mathrm{i}\epsilon\ell)\right)

The multiplication table of this basis is given by
