Remark 2.8
.

Note that
\operatorname{span}(f_{-1},f_{0},f_{1})
equals to
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\operatorname{span}(f_{-3},f_{-2},f_{2},f_{3})
equals
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
. With respect to the isomorphism
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\to\Im(\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathbb{H}
given by (
6
), we have

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 59.41673pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-59.41673pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{span}(f_{-2},f_{0},f_{2})=\Im(\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{C}}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 83.41673pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 129.2611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{span}(f_{-3},f_{-1},f_{1},f_{3})=\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{C}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

3.
Holomorphic curves in the 6-pseudosphere

Consider a smooth, connected and oriented surface
S
. In this section we introduce the notion of an
alternating holomorphic curve
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. These curves have a naturally defined Frenet framing which is used to give a holomorphic description of alternating holomorphic curves in Theorem
3.23
. We start by introducing relevant homogeneous spaces.

3.1.
Pseudospheres

Consider a real vector space
V
equipped with a non-degenerate quadratic form
Q
of signature
(p,q)
. The
signature
(p-1,q)
pseudosphere
is defined as

\mathbf{S}^{p-1,q}=\{z\in V~,~Q(z)=1\}~.

The tangent space to
\mathbf{S}^{p-1,q}
at a point
z
is naturally identified with

\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{S}^{p-1,q}=\{x\in V~,~\langle z,x\rangle=0\}=z^{\bot}~.

In particular, the quadratic form
Q
restricts to a signature
(p-1,q)
pseudo-Riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathbf{S}^{p-1,q}
. This metric has constant curvature
+1
and the orthogonal group
\mathsf{O}(V,Q)
acts by isometries on
(\mathbf{S}^{p-1,q},\mathbf{g})
.

Similarly, the
signature
(p,q-1)
pseudohyperbolic space
is the quadric

\mathbf{H}^{p,q-1}=\{z\in V~,~Q(z)=-1\}~.

In the same way as for the pseudosphere,
\mathbf{H}^{p,q-1}
inherits a signature
(p,q-1)
pseudo-Riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
of curvature
-1
.

Remark 3.1
.

Changing the quadratic form from
Q
to
-Q
defines an anti-isometry between
\mathbf{H}^{p,q-1}
and
\mathbf{S}^{q-1,p}
. As a result we will also refer to
\mathbf{H}^{p,q-1}
as a pseudosphere.

We will consider
(V,Q)=(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})
with
\mathbf{q}(z)=z\cdot z
. Since
\mathbf{q}
has signature
(4,3)
, the relevant pseudospheres are
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
.
The product
\times
on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
will induce extra
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant structures on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
.

3.2.
The space
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}

Consider the left multiplication map

(9)

L:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.79169pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.79169pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.30487pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.30487pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{End}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-5.54515pt\raise-7.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.54515pt\raise-7.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.79169pt\raise-7.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.79169pt\raise-7.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{z}:w\mapsto z\times w}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

For
z\in\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the kernel of
L_{z}
is spanned by
z
and, since the form
\Omega
from (
3
) is skew symmetric, the image of
L_{z}
is
z^{\bot}
. In particular,
L_{z}
restricts to an endomorphism of
\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathbf{J}:\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
be the endomorphism defined by
\mathbf{J}_{z}={L_{z}}_{|_{z^{\bot}}}:\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}\to\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Then
\mathbf{J}
defines a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant almost complex structure on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which is compatible with the metric
\mathbf{g}
.

Proof.

As
\mathbf{J}
is constructed using the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant product
\times
on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
,
\mathbf{J}
is
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant. Since
\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}=z^{\bot}
,
we have
\mathbf{J}_{z}(w)=z\times w=z\cdotp w
. By (
2
),
\mathbf{J}
is an almost complex structure:

\mathbf{J}_{z}^{2}(w)=z\cdot(z\cdot w)=(z\cdot z)\cdot w=-w~.

To see that
\mathbf{J}
preserves the metric
\mathbf{g},
we compute

\mathbf{q}(\mathbf{J}_{z}(w))=(z\cdot w)\cdot(z\cdot w)=z\cdot((w\cdot z)\cdot w)=-z\cdot((z\cdot w)\cdot w)=-z^{2}\cdot w^{2}=\mathbf{q}(w)~,

where we used that
z,w
span an associative subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
and
z\cdot w=z\times w=-w\cdot z
.
∎

We now show that
\mathbf{J}
is not integrable by computing
\nabla\mathbf{J}
, where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.
Since the map
L
from (
9
) is linear, its covariant derivative using the trivial flat connection
D
on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
is given by

(D_{X}L)_{z}(Y)=X(z)\times Y(z)~,

where
X,Y
are vector fields on
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
.
Since the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is induced by
D
and
\mathbf{J}
is the restriction of
L
to
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, we obtain

(10)

(\nabla_{X}\mathbf{J})_{z}(Y)=\big(X(z)\times Y(z)\big)^{z^{\bot}}

where
(~)^{z^{\bot}}
is the orthogonal projection of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
onto
z^{\bot}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
z
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and any nonzero
w
in
\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the restriction of
\times
to the complex line
\operatorname{span}(w,J_{z}(w))
is zero.

Proof.

Since
z,w
span an associative subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, we have

\mathbf{J}_{z}(w)\times w=\big((z\cdotp w)\cdotp w\big)^{z^{\bot}}=\big(z\cdotp(w\cdotp w)\big)^{z^{\bot}}=\big(\mathbf{q}(w)z\big)^{z^{\bot}}=0~.

∎

Consider now the non-degenerate
2
-form
\boldsymbol{\omega}
on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
defined by

\boldsymbol{\omega}(X,Y)=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{J}X,Y)~.

Since
\mathbf{J}
is non-integrable,
d\boldsymbol{\omega}\neq 0
. In fact,
d\boldsymbol{\omega}
is the restriction of the three from
\Omega
from (
3
) to
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
W,X,Y
be vector fields on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Then

d\boldsymbol{\omega}(W,X,Y)=\Omega(W,X,Y)=\mathbf{g}(W\times X,Y)~.

Proof.

By the definition of the exterior derivative,
3d\boldsymbol{\omega}(W,X,Y)
is given by

W(\boldsymbol{\omega}(X,Y))-X(\boldsymbol{\omega}(W,Y)+Y(\boldsymbol{\omega}(W,X))-\boldsymbol{\omega}([W,X],Y)+\boldsymbol{\omega}([W,Y],X)-\boldsymbol{\omega}([X,Y],W)~.

By definition we have

W(\boldsymbol{\omega}(X,Y))=W(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{J}X,Y))=\mathbf{g}((\nabla_{W}\mathbf{J})X,Y)+\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{J}(\nabla_{W}X),Y)+\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{J}X,\nabla_{W}Y)~.

Using equation (
10
) we have

W(g(\mathbf{J}X,Y))=\Omega(W,X,Y)+\boldsymbol{\omega}(\nabla_{W}X,Y)+\boldsymbol{\omega}(X,\nabla_{W}Y).

As the Levi-Civita connection is torsion free, adding the terms gives

d\boldsymbol{\omega}(W,X,Y)=\frac{1}{3}(\Omega(W,X,Y)-\Omega(X,W,Y)+\Omega(W,Y,X))=\Omega(W,X,Y)~.

∎

Considering
d\boldsymbol{\omega}
as a complex
3
-form on
(\mathbf{H}^{4,2},\mathbf{J})
, we can decompose it into types:

d\boldsymbol{\omega}=\theta+\zeta+\overline{\zeta}+\overline{\theta}~,

where
\theta
has type
(3,0)
and
\zeta
has type
(2,1)
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The
(3,0)
-form
\theta
is nowhere zero and the
(2,1)
-form
\zeta
is identically zero.

Proof.

The transitive action of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
preserves
\mathbf{J}
and
\Omega=d\boldsymbol{\omega}
, and hence preserves type decomposition of
d\boldsymbol{\omega}
. Thus, it suffices to compute
\theta
and
\zeta
at a point.

The imaginary octonion
z=j
has norm
-1
and hence
j\in\mathbf{H}^{4,2}.
Recall the basis
\{f_{-3},\cdots,f_{3}\}
of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})^{\mathbb{C}}
from (
7
). The complexified tangent space is given by

\mathrm{T}_{j}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}=j^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{C}=\operatorname{span}(f_{-3},f_{-2},f_{-1},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}).

The quadratic form
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{J}_{j}:\mathrm{T}^{\mathbb{C}}_{j}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathbb{C}}_{j}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
are given by (
8
). In particular,
\mathbf{J}
acts with eigenvalue
+\mathrm{i}
on
f_{-3},f_{1},f_{2}
and eigenvalue
-\mathrm{i}
on
f_{-2},f_{-1},f_{3}.

To see the form
\theta
is nonzero at
j\in\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
we compute

\theta_{j}(f_{-3},f_{1},f_{2})=\Omega(f_{-3},f_{1},f_{2})=\langle f_{-3}\times f_{1},f_{2}\rangle=-\sqrt{2}\langle f_{-2},f_{2}\rangle=\sqrt{2}.

For
\zeta_{j}
, it suffices to consider
\Omega(f_{a},f_{b},f_{c})
, where
a<b\in\{-3,1,2\}
and
c\in\{-2,-1,3\}.

We have

f_{-3}\times f_{1}=-\sqrt{2}f_{-2}\ ~\ ~,~\ ~\ f_{-3}\times f_{2}=-\sqrt{2}f_{-1}~\ \text{ and }~\ f_{1}\times f_{2}=\sqrt{2}f_{3}~,

which are all orthogonal to
\operatorname{span}(f_{-2},f_{-1},f_{3})
.
Hence
\zeta_{j}=0.

∎

Recall that the
canonical bundle
of
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is the determinant of the holomorphic cotangent bundle of
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Its sections are complex
(3,0)
-forms. Since
\theta
is never vanishing, we get

Corollary 3.6
.

The
(3,0)
-form
\theta
defines a trivialization canonical bundle of
(\mathbf{H}^{4,2},\mathbf{J})
.

Corollary 3.7
.

The
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-stabilizer of a point
z\in\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SU}(2,1)
.

Proof.

For
z\in\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(z)
preserves the signature
(2,1)
hermitian form
\mathbf{h}_{z}=\mathbf{g}_{z}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{z}
. Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
also preserves the volume form
\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}
, we have
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(z)<\mathsf{SU}(2,1)
. Equality follows from connectedness of
\mathsf{SU}(2,1)
and a dimension count. Namely,
\dim(\mathbf{H}^{4,2})=6
and
\dim(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})=14
, so
\dim(\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(z))=8=\dim(\mathsf{SU}(2,1)).

∎

3.3.
The space
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}

Recall that an
almost paracomplex structure
on a manifold
M
is an endomorphism
\psi:\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}M
such that
\psi^{2}=\mathrm{Id}
and
\dim(\ker(\mathrm{Id}-\psi))=\dim(\ker(\mathrm{Id}+\psi))
. A pseudo-Riemannian metric
g
on
M
is
compatible
with
\psi
if
\psi
is an anti-isometry of
g
.
Such a
g
must have neutral signature and the distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\pm}=\ker(\psi\mp\mathrm{Id})
define a pair of transverse half dimensional isotropic subspace. The subgroup of
\mathsf{O}(n,n)
which preserves a pair of transverse half dimensional isotropic subspaces is isomorphic to
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
, where the corresponding representation on
\mathcal{D}^{+}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}
are dual to each other.

For each
z\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
, the multiplication map (
9
) defines an endomorphism of
L_{z}:\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}\to\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\psi:\mathrm{T}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
be the endomorphism defined by
\psi_{z}=L_{z}:\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}\to\mathrm{T}_{z}\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
. Then
\psi
defines a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-invariant paracomplex structure on
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
which is compatible with the metric
\mathbf{g}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
3.2
with the exception that for
z\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
we have
z\cdot z=+1.

∎

Each point
z\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
defines two transverse isotropic
3
-planes
\mathcal{D}^{\pm}_{z}\subset z^{\perp}\subset\Im(\mathbb{O})
. The antipodal map on
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
exchanges
\mathcal{D}^{+}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}
.
Denote the quotient of
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
by the antipodal map by
\mathbf{P}_{+}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))
(that is,
\mathbf{P}_{+}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))
is the space of positive definite lines in
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
).

Lemma 3.9
.

The restriction of the
3
-form
\Omega
to the distribution
\mathcal{D}^{+}
is nowhere zero.

Proof.

The action
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
is transitive and preserves the three form
\Omega.
Hence, it suffices to compute
\Omega
at any point of
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}.
In particular, the imaginary octonion
\delta
has norm
+1
. Hence,
\delta\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
and
\psi_{\delta}=\delta\times(\cdotp):\delta^{\bot}\to\delta^{\bot}
.

Using the notation
z=x+y\ell
from Section
2.2
we compute

\textstyle{\delta\cdot(j-\epsilon)=-\epsilon+j~,}
\textstyle{\delta\cdot(\ell+\delta\ell)=\delta\ell+\ell}
and
\textstyle{\delta\cdot(j\ell+\epsilon\ell)=\epsilon\ell+j\ell~.}

Thus,
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}^{+}=\operatorname{span}(j-\epsilon,\ell+\delta\ell,j\ell+\epsilon\ell)
.
As
(j-\epsilon)\cdotp(\ell+\delta\ell)=2(j\ell-\epsilon\ell)~,
the restriction of
\Omega
to
\mathcal{D}_{\delta}^{+}
is given by

\Omega(j-\epsilon,\ell+\delta\ell,j\ell+\epsilon\ell)=2\langle j\ell-\epsilon\ell,j\ell+\epsilon\ell\rangle=-4.

We conclude that
\Omega_{\delta}
is nonzero.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

The
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-stabilizer of a point
z\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

For
z\in\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
, the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(z)
preserves the distributions
\mathcal{D}^{+}_{z}\oplus\mathcal{D}^{-}_{z}
. Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
preserves the
3
-form
\Omega
, and
\Omega|_{\mathcal{D}^{+}_{z}}
is nonzero, we have
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(z)<\mathsf{SL}(\mathcal{D}_{z}^{+})
. As in Corollary
3.7
, equality follows from connectedness of
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and a dimension count.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Since the antipodal map exchanges the
\pm
distributions, the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-stabilizer of a point in
[x]\in\mathbf{P}_{+}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}))
is isomorphic to the unique nonsplit extension

1\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}~.

3.4.
Alternating holomorphic curves into
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}

Let
S
be a connected oriented surface.

Definition 3.12
.

A
holomorphic curve
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is an immersion
f:S\to(\mathbf{H}^{4,2},\mathbf{J})
such that the tangent bundle of
S
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant, that is

\mathbf{J}(df(\mathrm{T}S))=df(\mathrm{T}S)~,

and the corresponding action of
\mathbf{J}
on
\mathrm{T}S
is orientation preserving.

Given a holomorphic curve
f
, there is a unique complex structure
\mathsf{j}
on
S
such that

df\circ\mathsf{j}=\mathbf{J}\circ df.

We will refer to
\mathsf{j}
as
the induced complex structure
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Holomorphic curves into
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
are analytic.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
acts analytically on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the almost-complex structure
\mathbf{J}
depends analytically on the base point. Moreover, holomorphic curves into
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
are solution to the Cauchy-Riemann equation
df\circ\mathsf{j}=\mathbf{J}\circ df
, which is a non-linear elliptic equation.
The result then follows from the main result of
[
26
]
: solutions to a non-linear elliptic equations with analytic coefficients are analytic.
∎

Given a holomorphic curve
f
from
S
into
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the pullback bundle
f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is equipped with the pullback metric, connection and complex structure that we still denote by
\mathbf{g},~\nabla
and
\mathbf{J}
respectively.

A holomorphic curve
f
is called
spacelike
if the induced metric on
S
is Riemannian. In this case, identifying
\mathrm{T}S
with
df(\mathrm{T}S)\subset f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, there is
\mathbf{g}
-orthogonal splitting

f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}=\mathrm{T}S\oplus(\mathrm{T}S)^{\bot}~.

Since the almost complex structure
\mathbf{J}
is compatible with the metric
\mathbf{g},
this splitting is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant.
The
second fundamental form
of
f
is the
1
-form
\mathrm{II}\in\Omega^{1}(S,\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{T}S,(\mathrm{T}S)^{\bot}))

defined by

\mathrm{II}(X,Y)=(\nabla_{X}Y)^{\bot}~.

Recall that
\mathrm{II}
is symmetric and that
f
is totally geodesic if and only if
\mathrm{II}=0.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
be a spacelike holomorphic curve with induced complex structure
\mathsf{j}
and second fundamental form
\mathrm{II}
. Then for any vector fields
X,Y
on
S
, we have

\mathrm{II}(\mathsf{j}(X),Y)=\mathrm{II}(X,\mathsf{j}(Y))=\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(X,Y))~.

Proof.

By definition we have

\mathrm{II}(X,\mathsf{j}(Y))=\big(\nabla_{X}(\mathbf{J}(Y))\big)^{\bot}=\big((\nabla_{X}\mathbf{J})(Y)+\mathbf{J}(\nabla_{X}Y)\big)^{\bot}~.

Since
X
are
Y
contained in the complex line, Lemma
3.3
implies
X\times Y=0.

Thus,
(\nabla_{X}\mathbf{J})(Y)=0
by equation (
10
).
Since
\mathbf{J}
is compatible with the metric, we have

\mathrm{II}(X,\mathsf{j}(Y))=(\mathbf{J}(\nabla_{X}Y))^{\bot}=\mathbf{J}(\nabla_{X}Y)^{\bot}=\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(X,Y))~.

Since
\mathrm{II}
is symmetric, we have

\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(X,Y))=\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(Y,X))=\mathrm{II}(Y,\mathsf{j}(X))=\mathrm{II}(\mathsf{j}(X),Y)~.

∎

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
f
be a spacelike holomorphic curve which is not totally geodesic. Then

(1)

f
has vanishing mean curvature, and

(2)

there is a unique
\mathbf{J}
-invariant rank 2 subbundle
\mathrm{N}S\subset(\mathrm{T}S)^{\bot}
such that the image of
\mathrm{II}
lies in
\mathrm{N}S
.

We will call the bundle
\mathrm{N}S
the
normal bundle of
f
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{1},X_{2})
be a local orthonormal framing of
\mathrm{T}S
with
\mathsf{j}(X_{1})=X_{2}
.
Lemma
3.14
gives

\mathrm{II}(X_{2},X_{2})=\mathrm{II}(X_{2},\mathsf{j}(X_{1}))=\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(X_{2},X_{1}))=\mathbf{J}^{2}(\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{1}))=-\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{1})~.

In particular, the trace of
\mathrm{II}
vanishes, and so the mean curvature of
f
is zero.

For item
(2)
, there is an open set
U\subset S
on which the image of
\mathrm{II}
has maximal dimension. Locally, the image of
\mathrm{II}
is spanned by
\{\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{1}),\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{2}),\mathrm{II}(X_{2},X_{2})\}
.
The image of
\mathrm{II}
has rank 2 on
U
since
\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{1})=-\mathrm{II}(X_{2},X_{2})=-\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{II}(X_{1},X_{2}))
and
f
is not totally geodesic.

We claim that
U
is dense. If not were not dense, there would be an open set on which the rank of
\mathrm{II}
is
0
, meaning that
f(S)
would be totally geodesic on some open space. But holomorphic curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
are analytic by Lemma
3.13
, so
f(S)
would be totally geodesic everywhere. Thus the image of
\mathrm{II}
defines a complex line bundle on a dense open set, which thus extends to a complex line bundle
\mathrm{N}S
on
S
.
∎

We now define the notion of a alternating holomorphic curve in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}.

Definition 3.16
.

An alternating holomorphic curve is a holomorphic curve
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which is spacelike, not totally geodesic, and has negative-definite normal bundle
\mathrm{N}S
.

We define the
binormal bundle

\mathrm{B}S
of an alternating holomorphic map
f
to be the rank two bundle

\mathrm{B}S=(\mathrm{T}S\oplus\mathrm{N}S)^{\perp}.

Hence, an alternating holomorphic curve defines an orthogonal splitting

f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}=\mathrm{T}S\oplus\mathrm{N}S\oplus\mathrm{B}S.

Since
\mathbf{J}
preserves
\mathbf{g},

\mathrm{T}S
and
\mathrm{N}S
,
\mathbf{J}
also preserves the binormal bundle
\mathrm{B}S
.

Remark 3.17
.

In
[
27
]
, Nie defines the notion of an A-surface in
\mathbf{H}^{p,q}
. Such surfaces are assumed to be spacelike and have rank two “higher” normal bundles with alternating signature. In particular, for
A
-curves in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
the image of the second fundamental form
\mathrm{II}
is assumed to define a rank two subbundle which is assumed to be negative definite.
Thus, alternating holomorphic curves define
A
-surfaces in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.

3.5.
Frenet Framing

The trivial bundle
\underline{\Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\mathbf{H}^{4,2}\times\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
has a tautological section
\sigma
which defines a splitting
\underline{\Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, where
\sigma(x)=(1,0)
. In this splitting, the metric
g_{\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})}=-1\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{H}^{4,2}}
, and the trivial connection
\underline{D}
decomposes as

\underline{D}=\begin{pmatrix}d&\mathbb{1}^{*}\\
\mathbb{1}&\nabla\end{pmatrix},

where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
,
\mathbb{1}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbf{H}^{4,2},\operatorname{Hom}(\underline{\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}))
is the identity and
\mathbb{1}^{*}
is the adjoint of with respect to the metric
g_{\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})}
.
By construction, the product with the tautological section
\sigma
defines the almost complex
\mathbf{J}
on
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
,

(1,0)\times(u,v)=(0,\mathbf{J}(v)).

Given a map
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, let
(E,D,\mathbf{g},\times)
denote the pullback of the trivial bundle
\underline{\Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
, the trivial connection
\underline{D}
, the signature
(4,3)
metric
g_{\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})}
and the product
\times:\Lambda^{2}\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})\to\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
. We have the following decompositions

E=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus f^{*}T\mathbf{H}^{4,2}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ D=\begin{pmatrix}d&df^{*}\\
df&f^{*}\nabla\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ and\ \ \ \ \ \mathbf{g}=-1\oplus f^{*}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{H}^{4,2}}.

The map
f
is identified with the pullback of the tautological section
f^{*}\sigma:S\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}
, and
(1,0)\times(u,v)=(0,f^{*}\mathbf{J}(v)).

Definition 3.18
.

When
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is an alternating holomorphic curve,
E=f^{*}\underline{\Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
decomposes as

(11)

E=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathrm{T}S\oplus\mathrm{N}S\oplus\mathrm{B}S.

We call this decomposition, the Frenet framing of
f.

In the Frenet framing, the metric decomposes as
\mathbf{g}=-1\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}}\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}}
, where
1\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}\oplus(-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}})\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}}
is positive definite, and the almost complex structure decomposes as
\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}}\oplus\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{N}}\oplus\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{B}}
. In particular,

(12)

(1,0,0,0)\times(a,t,n,b)=(0,\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}}(t),\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{N}}(n),\mathbf{J}_{B}(b)).

Also, in the Frenet framing the connection
D
decomposes as

(13)

D=\begin{pmatrix}d&\mathbb{1}^{*}&0&0\\
\mathbb{1}&\nabla^{\mathrm{T}}&S_{2}&0\\
0&\mathrm{II}&\nabla^{\mathrm{N}}&S_{3}\\
0&0&\mathrm{III}&\nabla^{\mathrm{B}}\end{pmatrix}

where:

•

\nabla^{\mathrm{T}},~\nabla^{\mathrm{N}},~\nabla^{\mathrm{B}}
are metric connections on
(\mathrm{T}S,\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}},\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}}),~(\mathrm{N}S,-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}},\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{N}}),~(\mathrm{B}S,\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{B}})
, respectively; they are unitary with respect to the induced hermitian metrics.

•

\mathbb{1}\in\Omega^{1}(\underline{\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{T}S)
is the identity between the tangent bundle of
S
and
\mathrm{T}S\cong df(\mathrm{T}S)\subset f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
.

•

\mathrm{II}\in\Omega^{1}(S,\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{T}S,\mathrm{N}S))
is the second fundamental form of
f
.

•

\mathrm{III}\in\Omega^{1}(S,\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{N}S,\mathrm{B}S))
will be called the
third fundamental form
of
f
.

•

S_{2}\in\Omega^{1}(S,\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{N}S,\mathrm{T}S))
and
S_{3}\in\Omega^{1}(S,\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{B}S,\mathrm{N}S))
satisfy

\textstyle{-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}}(\mathrm{II}(X,Y),\nu)=\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}(Y,S_{2}(X,\nu))~}
and
\textstyle{\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(X,\nu),\beta)=-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}}(\nu,S_{3}(X,\beta))~,}

where
X,Y
are vector fields on
S
and
\nu,\beta
are sections of
\mathrm{N}S
and
\mathrm{B}S
, respectively.

Lemma 3.19
.

The tensors
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{III}
,
S_{2},
and
S_{3}
are all covariantly constant.

Proof.

We show
\mathrm{III}
is covariantly constant, the computations for
\mathrm{II}
,
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
are similar. This follows from the Codazzi equations in this context.
As
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
has constant sectional curvature, its curvature tensor
R
satisfies
R(X,Y)(\nu)=0
. Hence,

0=\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(R(X,Y)\nu)=\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\nu-\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}\nu-\nabla_{[X,Y]}\nu)

A computation using the Frenet framing (
13
) shows

\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\nu)=\mathrm{III}(X,\nabla_{Y}\nu)+\nabla_{X}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(Y,\nu)).

Thus,

0=\mathrm{III}(X,\nabla_{Y}\nu)+\nabla_{X}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(Y,\nu))-\mathrm{III}(Y,\nabla_{X}\nu)+\nabla_{Y}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(X,\nu)-\mathrm{III}([X,Y],\nu)

=\nabla_{X}^{\mathrm{N}\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(Y,\nu))-\nabla_{Y}^{\mathrm{N}\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{III}(X,\nu))-\mathrm{III}([X,Y],\nu)=2(\nabla^{\mathrm{N}\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{III})(X,Y,\nu).

where
\nabla^{\mathrm{N}\mathrm{B}}
is the induced connection on
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{N}S,\mathrm{B}S).

∎

Lemma 3.20
.

The tensor
\mathrm{III}(\cdot,\mathrm{II}(\cdot,\cdot))
is symmetric.

Proof.

Let
X,Y,Z
be vector fields on
S.
Since
\mathrm{II}(Y,Z)=\mathrm{II}(Z,Y)
, it suffices to compute

\mathrm{III}(X,\mathrm{II}(Y,Z))-\mathrm{III}(Y,\mathrm{II}(X,Z))~=~\pi_{\mathrm{B}}\big(\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}Z-\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}Z\big)~=~\pi_{\mathrm{B}}\big(R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z\big)~.

Since
\nabla
is the pull-back of a constant curvature metric,
R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z\in\Omega^{0}(S,\mathrm{T}S)
. Moreover,
\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z\in\Omega^{0}(S,\mathrm{T}S\oplus\mathrm{N}S)
so
\pi_{\mathrm{B}}\big(R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z\big)=0
.
∎

3.6.
Complexified Frenet framing

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
be an alternating holomorphic curve, and let
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
be the Riemann surface defined by the induced complex structure. The complexification of the Frenet framing of
f
is given by

E_{\mathbb{C}}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathrm{B}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{B}^{\prime\prime},

where
\mathrm{T}_{\mathbb{C}}S=\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}
is the decomposition of the complexification of
\mathrm{T}S
into
\pm\mathrm{i}
-eigenspaces of the complex linear extension of
\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}}
, similarly
\mathrm{N}_{\mathbb{C}}S=\mathrm{N}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{C}}S=\mathrm{B}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{B}^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

In the complexified Frenet framing, the product
\times
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

For the section
s_{0}=(1,0,\cdots,0)
of
E_{\mathbb{C}}
, the endomorphism
s_{0}\times(\cdot)
is given by

s_{0}\times(\cdot)=\mathrm{diag}(0,+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}).

(2)

For a sections
s^{\prime}=(t^{\prime},b^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{B}^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}=(t^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
of
\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathrm{B}^{\prime\prime}
, we have

s^{\prime}\times s^{\prime\prime}=t^{\prime}\times t^{\prime\prime}+n^{\prime}\times n^{\prime\prime}+b^{\prime}\times b^{\prime\prime}=-\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathbb{C}}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})+\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime})+\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\mathbb{C}}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}))s_{0}~.

(3)

The product defines isomorphisms
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{B}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\mathrm{B}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathrm{N}^{\prime}.

Proof.

The first point follows from Equation (
12
) and the definitions of the
\pm\mathrm{i}
-eigenspaces. The remaining points follow Lemma
3.5
and the fact that
\mathbf{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(s_{0},s_{0})=-1.

∎

The complex linear extension
\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}\in\Omega^{1}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{N}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}))
is given by

\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{II}^{\prime}&\overline{\mathrm{II}}^{\prime\prime}\\
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}&\overline{\mathrm{II}}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathrm{II}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},\mathrm{N}^{\prime}))
,
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime},\mathrm{N}^{\prime\prime}))
, and
\overline{\mathrm{II}}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathrm{II}}^{\prime\prime}
are the conjugate maps. The tensors
\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}},~(S_{2})_{\mathbb{C}},
and
(S_{3})_{\mathbb{C}}
have analogous decompositions.

Proposition 3.22
.

With the above notation, we have

(1)

\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}=0
and
S_{2}^{\prime\prime}=0,
and

(2)

for any vector field
X
on
S
and sections
\nu,\beta
of
\mathrm{N}^{\prime}S,\mathrm{B}^{\prime}S
, respectively we have

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 51.16199pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-51.16199pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{III}^{\prime\prime}(X,\nu)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(X\times\nu)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 75.16199pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 121.00635pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S_{3}^{\prime\prime}(X,\beta)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(X\times\beta)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

Proof.

Note that
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}=0
if and only if
\mathrm{II}\circ\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}}=\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{N}}\circ\mathrm{II}
. So the first item is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.14
and the duality between
S_{2}
and
\mathrm{II}
.

For the second item, denote the orthogonal projection onto
\mathrm{B}S
by
\pi_{\mathrm{B}}
, note that
\pi_{\mathrm{B}}
commutes with
\mathbf{J}.
By definition,
\mathrm{III}(X,\nu)=\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(\nabla_{X}\nu),
and

\mathrm{III}^{\prime\prime}(X,\nu)=\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}(X,\nu)+\mathrm{i}\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}(X,\nu)))~.

Using equation (
10
),
\mathbf{J}(\nu)=\mathrm{i}\nu
and
\mathbf{J}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{B}}=\pi_{\mathrm{B}}\circ\mathbf{J}
, we have

\mathrm{i}\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}(X,\nu))=\mathbf{J}(\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}(X,\mathbf{J}\nu))=\mathbf{J}(\pi_{\mathrm{B}}((\nabla_{X}\mathbf{J})(\nu)+\mathbf{J}(\nabla_{X}\nu)))

=\mathbf{J}(\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(X\times\nu))-\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}(X,\nu).

Thus
\mathrm{III}^{\prime\prime}(X,\nu)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{J}(\pi_{\mathrm{B}}(X\times\nu)).
By Lemma
3.21
,
X\times\nu\in\mathrm{B}^{\prime\prime}
since
X\in\mathrm{T}S
and
\nu\in\mathrm{N}^{\prime}
. Hence
\mathrm{III}^{\prime\prime}(X,\nu)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(X\times\nu).

The computation for
S_{3}
now follows from duality between
\mathrm{III}
and
S_{3}
.
∎

The hermitian metric on
(\mathrm{T}S,g_{\mathrm{T}},\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{T}})
induces a hermitian metric
h_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}\oplus h_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
on
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}
. The complex linear extension of
\nabla^{T}
induces hermitian connections
\nabla^{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}
and
\nabla^{\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
on
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. The
(0,1)
-part of these connections define holomorphic structures
\bar{\partial}_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}
and
\bar{\partial}_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}}
on
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{T}
and
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
denote the associated holomorphic line bundles on the Riemann surface
X.

Since
\mathcal{T}
and
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
are isotropic with respect to
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the hermitian metric defines an isomorphism between
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
and the dual holomorphic line bundle
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
; under this identification

(\mathcal{T}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathbb{C}})=\left(\mathcal{T}\oplus\mathcal{T}^{-1},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\right).

Repeating the above discussion for the bundles
\mathrm{N}_{\mathbb{C}}S
and
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{C}}S
, we see that the
(0,1)
-part of the complex linear extension of the metric connections
d,\nabla^{\mathrm{T}},\nabla^{\mathrm{N}},\nabla^{\mathrm{B}}
define a holomorphic structure
\bar{\partial}_{E_{\mathbb{C}}}
on
E_{\mathbb{C}}
which decomposes as

(14)

\mathcal{E}=(E_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\partial}_{E_{\mathbb{C}}})=\mathcal{O}_{X}\oplus\mathcal{T}\oplus\mathcal{T}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1},

where
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the trivial holomorphic line bundle. With respect to this decomposition, the complex linear extension of the positive definite metric and the Hermitian metric are given by

(1\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}\oplus(-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}})\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}})^{\mathbb{C}}=1\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

h=h_{0}\oplus h_{\mathcal{T}}\oplus h_{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}\oplus h_{\mathcal{N}}\oplus h_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}\oplus h_{\mathcal{B}}\oplus h_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}.

As the isomorphisms
\mathcal{T}^{-1}\cong\overline{\mathcal{T}}
,
\mathcal{N}^{-1}\cong\overline{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\cong\overline{\mathcal{B}}
are given by the Hermitian metric, the second form decompose as

\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{II}^{\prime}&(\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime})^{*_{h}}\\
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}&(\mathrm{II}^{\prime})^{*_{h}}\end{pmatrix},

where
(\mathrm{II}^{\prime})^{*_{h}},(\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime})^{*_{h}}
are the Hermitian adjionts of
\mathrm{II}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}
. The tensors,
\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}},

S_{2}
and
S_{3}
decompose similarly.

In the complex Frenet framing, the flat connection
D
decomposes as follows.

Theorem 3.23
.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
be an alternating holomorphic curve and
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
be the induced Riemann surface. Let
(E,D)=(f^{*}\underline{\Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),f^{*}\underline{D})
be the pullback of the trivial bundle with trivial connection on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}.
Then, with respect to the complexified Frenet framing

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\oplus\mathcal{T}\oplus\mathcal{T}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1},

the flat connection
D
decomposes a

(15)

D=\begin{pmatrix}d&1^{*_{h}}&1&0&0&0&0\\
1&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{T}}}&0&\beta^{*_{h}}&0&0&0\\
1^{*_{h}}&0&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}}&0&\beta&0&0\\
0&\beta&0&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{N}}}&0&\delta^{*_{h}}&\alpha^{*_{h}}\\
0&0&\beta^{*_{h}}&0&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}}&\alpha&\delta\\
0&0&0&\delta&\alpha^{*_{h}}&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{B}}}&0\\
0&0&0&\alpha&\delta^{*_{h}}&0&\nabla_{h_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}}\end{pmatrix},

where

•

1\in\Omega^{1,0}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{T}))
is the identity between
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}X
and
\partial f(\mathrm{T}^{\prime}X)\cong\mathcal{T}\subset f^{*}(\mathrm{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}),

•

\beta\in\Omega^{1,0}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{N}))
is holomorphic,

•

\delta\in\Omega^{1,0}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}))
is holomorphic,

•

\alpha\in\Omega^{1,0}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{N}^{-1}))
is nonzero, holomorphic and given by
\alpha(b)(Y)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}b\times Y,
for any section
b
of
\mathcal{B}
and vector field
Y
.

•

1^{*_{h}},~\beta^{*_{h}},~\delta^{*_{h}}
and
\alpha^{*_{h}}
are the hermitian adjionts of
1,~\beta,~\delta
and
\alpha
, respectively.

Remark 3.24
.

The line bundles
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
are the holomorphic tangent and cotangent bundles of the Riemann surface
X
, respectively. Dropping the immersion assumption in the definition of an alternating holomorphic curves would give the same type of framing with
\mathcal{T}
replaced by
\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{D}}
, where
\mathsf{D}\subset X
is the divisor where the differential
\partial f
vanishes.

Proof.

The flat connection in the Frenet framing is given by complex linearly extending the expression (
13
).
The term
\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}}\in\Omega^{1}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{T}\oplus\mathcal{T}^{-1}))
is the identification of
d_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime}S\oplus\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}S)=\mathcal{T}\oplus\mathcal{T}^{-1}\subset f^{*}\mathrm{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. As
f
is a holomorphic immersion,
\mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{C}}=1\in\Omega^{1,0}(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{T})).

For the second fundamental form
\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathrm{II}^{\prime\prime}=0
by Proposition
3.22
. The tensor
\beta
is
\mathrm{II}^{\prime}
; it is a
(1,0)
-form by Lemma
3.14
. Namely,

\mathrm{II}^{\prime}(Y,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}(Y,t^{\prime})-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{J}\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}(Y,t^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}(Y,t^{\prime})-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{II}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathsf{j}(Y),t^{\prime})),

and
\mathrm{II}^{\prime}(Y,t^{\prime})
vanishes when
\mathsf{j}(Y)=-\mathrm{i}Y
. Part 3 of Proposition
3.22
implies
\beta
is holomorphic.

The tensor
\alpha
is
\mathrm{III}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
; it has type
(1,0)
since
\mathrm{T}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathrm{N}^{\prime}=0
. The tensor
\delta
is
\mathrm{III}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
. Generically on the Riemann surface, we have
\mathrm{III}^{\prime}(Y,\nu)=\mathrm{III}^{\prime}(Y,\mathrm{II}^{\prime}(Z,\tau))
, for
\tau
a section of
\mathcal{T}.
By Lemma
3.20

\mathrm{III}^{\prime}(Y,\nu)=\mathrm{III}^{\prime}(Y,\mathrm{II}^{\prime}(Z,\tau))=\mathrm{III}^{\prime}(Z,\mathrm{II}^{\prime}(Y,\tau)).

Hence,
\mathrm{III}^{\prime}
is a
(1,0)
-form since
\mathrm{II}^{\prime}
is a
(1,0)
-form. Part 3 of Proposition
3.22
implies
\delta
is holomorphic.
To conclude, note that
(S_{2})_{\mathbb{C}}
and
(S_{3})_{\mathbb{C}}
are given by

(S_{2})_{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta&0\\
0&\beta^{*_{h}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ (S_{3})_{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\alpha\\
\alpha^{*_{h}}&\delta^{*_{h}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

∎

The group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
acts on the set of alternating holomorphic curves by
(g\cdot f)(x)=g\cdot f(x).
This action does not change the induced Riemann surface, but it can change the Frenet framing of Theorem
3.23
slightly.
Namely,
if
(\mathcal{E},h)
and
(\hat{\mathcal{E}},\hat{h})
are the holomorphic bundles with induced hermitian metrics associated to
f
and
g\cdot f
, respectively, then
f^{*}g
defines an isomorphism
(\mathcal{E},h)\cong(\hat{\mathcal{E}},\hat{h})
.
Under this isomorphism, the holomorphic tangent bundles are the same and the holomorphic normal and binormal bundles are identified
(\mathcal{N}_{,}h_{\mathcal{N}})\cong(\hat{\mathcal{N}},\hat{h}_{\hat{\mathcal{N}}})
and
(\mathcal{B},h_{\mathcal{B}})\cong(\hat{\mathcal{B}},\hat{h}_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}})
. Since such an isomorphism preserves the product, and the product identifies
\mathcal{N}\cong\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{T}
, the space of such identifications is a
\mathsf{U}(1)
-torsor.
Under such an isomorphism, there is
\lambda\in\mathsf{U}(1)
such that the associated holomorphic sections
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
from (
15
) are changed by

(16)

(\alpha,\beta,\delta)\mapsto(\alpha,\lambda\beta,\lambda^{-2}\delta)~.

4.
Higgs bundles and equivariant alternating holomorphic curves

Fix a Riemann surface
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
and denote its holomorphic cotangent bundle by
\mathcal{K}.
We do not assume
X
is compact until §
5
. In this section we introduce the basics of Higgs bundles and harmonic bundles that are relevant to this paper. We then explain how certain
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles determine and are determined by alternating holomorphic curves which are equivariant with respect to a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}.

We start by recalling some Lie theory.

4.1.
Cartan decompositions

Every semisimple real Lie group
\mathsf{G}
has a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}
which is unique up to conjugation, and
\mathsf{G}
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathsf{K}
. A choice of maximal compact subgroup defines an Lie algebra involution
\theta:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
a called a Cartan involution. The
+1
-eigenspace of the Cartan involution is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
of the maximal compact
\mathsf{K}
and the
(-1)
-eigenspace
\mathfrak{p}
is the subspace perpendicular to
\mathfrak{k}
with respect to the Killing form. Hence a choice of maximal compact defines a
Cartan decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
. A Cartan decomposition satisfies the bracket relations

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.415pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.415pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}]\subset\mathfrak{k}~,}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.415pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}]\subset\mathfrak{p}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 112.6928pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 158.80394pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}]\subset\mathfrak{k}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Complexifying, defines the
complexified Cartan decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Remark 4.1
.

If
\mathsf{G}_{0}<\mathsf{G}
is a reductive subgroup, then we can choose a maximal compact
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
such that
\mathsf{K}_{0}=\mathsf{G}_{0}\cap\mathsf{K}
is a maximal compact of
\mathsf{G}_{0}
. Conversely, if
\mathsf{K}_{0}<\mathsf{G}_{0}
is a maximal compact subgroup, we can extend it to a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G},
see for example
[
1
, §3]
.

If
(E,\mathbf{q})
is a
7
-dimensional real vector space equipped with a signature
(4,3)
quadratic form, then the maximal compact of
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(E,\mathbf{q})\cong\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SO}(4)\times\mathsf{SO}(3)
. A choice of maximal compact is given by choosing an orthogonal splitting
E=U\oplus V
such that the restriction
\mathbf{q}_{U}
and
\mathbf{q}_{V}
of
\mathbf{q}
to
U
and
V
have signature
(4,0)
and
(0,3)
, respectively. With respect to this splitting, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(4,3)
is

\mathfrak{so}(4,3)=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}A&\eta^{\dagger}\\
\eta&B\end{pmatrix}~|~A\in\mathfrak{so}(U,\mathbf{q}_{U}),~B\in\mathfrak{so}(V,\mathbf{q}_{V}),~\eta\in\operatorname{Hom}(U,V),~\eta^{\dagger}=-\mathbf{q}_{U}^{-1}\eta^{T}\mathbf{q}_{V}\right\}~.

Thus, the Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{so}(4,3)
is

(17)

\mathfrak{so}(4,3)\cong\big(\mathfrak{so}(U,\mathbf{q}_{U})\oplus\mathfrak{so}(V,\mathbf{q}_{V})\big)\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(U,V).

By Lemma
2.6
, the maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SO}(4)
. A choice of maximal compact is given by choosing a decomposition
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})=\Im(\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathbb{H}
, where the multiplication is given by (
5
). The corresponding Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
is then given by intersecting with the Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{so}(4,3)=\mathfrak{so}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q}).

Specifically, if

(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})=(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{q}_{\mathbb{H}})\oplus(\Im(\mathbb{H}),\mathbf{q}_{\Im(\mathbb{H})})=(U,\mathbf{q}_{U})\oplus(V,\mathbf{q}_{V}),

then the Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
is given by
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p},
where

\mathfrak{k}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}~|~A\in\mathfrak{so}(U,\mathbf{q}_{U})~,~B\in\mathfrak{so}(V,\mathbf{q}_{V})~,~X(z_{1}\times z_{2})=X(z_{1})\times z_{2}+z_{1}\times X(z_{2})\right\}

\mathfrak{p}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}&\eta^{\dagger}\\
\eta&\end{pmatrix}~|~\eta^{\dagger}=-\mathbf{q}_{U}^{-1}\eta^{T}\mathbf{q}_{V}~,~X(z_{1}\times z_{2})=X(z_{1})\times z_{2}+z_{1}\times X(z_{2})\right\}~.

4.2.
Higgs bundles definitions

Let
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
be a Riemann surface. Fix a maximal compact subgroup
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{G}
and consider the Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
of the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{G}
. Let
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}<\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complexification of
\mathsf{K}
and denote the complexified Cartan decomposition by
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Note that
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
acts on
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}
; given a principal
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle
P\to X
we denote
P[\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}]
the associated vector bundle
P\times_{\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}
with fiber
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 4.2
.

With the above notation, a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle on
X
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)
, where

•

\mathcal{P}
is a holomorphic principal
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle over
X
and

•

\Phi
is a holomorphic section of
\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}]\otimes\mathcal{K}
called the Higgs field.

If we fix the underlying smooth
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle
P
, then two
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)
and
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
are isomorphic if differ by the action of the
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-gauge group. That is, if there is a smooth bundle isomorphism of
P
which defines an holomorphic isomorphism
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)\cong(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime}).

A complex group
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a real form of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In this case,
\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-Higgs bundle is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a holomorphic
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle on
X
and
\Phi
is a holomorphic section of the adjoint bundle
\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}]\otimes\mathcal{K}
.
Using faithful representations of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we can work with vector bundles. We give a few relevant examples:

Example 1
.

An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle on
X
is a tuple
(\mathcal{E},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
, where

•

\mathcal{E}\to X
is a holomorphic vector bundle of rank
n
,

•

\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{E}}:\Lambda^{n}\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a holomorphic isomorphism, and

•

\Phi
is a holomorphic section of
\mathsf{End}(\mathcal{E})\otimes\mathcal{K}
with
\operatorname{tr}(\Phi)=0.

Similarly, an
\mathsf{SO}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is an
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
Higgs bundle equipped with a nondegenerate holomorphic section
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
of the symmetric power
S^{2}\mathcal{E}^{*}
such that
\Phi^{T}Q_{\mathcal{E}}+Q_{\mathcal{E}}\Phi=0,
where
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
is viewed an isomorphism
Q_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{*}
and
\Phi^{T}:\mathcal{E}^{*}\to\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{K}
.

Example 2
.

The complexified Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{so}(4,3)
is given by complexifying (
17
).
An
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle is thus a tuple
(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{V}},\eta)
, where

•

(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{U}})
and
(\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{V}})
are respectively rank
4
and
3
holomorphic vector bundles equipped with holomorphic orthogonal structures
Q_{\mathcal{U}},Q_{\mathcal{V}}
and volume forms
\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{U}},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{V}}
and

•

\eta:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is a holomorphic bundle map.

The associated
\mathsf{SO}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is
(\mathcal{E},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{E}},Q_{\mathcal{E}},\Phi)=(\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V},\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge\operatorname{vol}_{\mathcal{V}},Q_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus-Q_{\mathcal{V}},\Phi),
where for
\eta^{\dagger}=Q_{\mathcal{U}}^{-1}\circ\eta^{T}\circ Q_{\mathcal{V}}
we have

(18)

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta^{\dagger}\\
\eta&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V}\to(\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V})\otimes\mathcal{K}~.

We now describe
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles. Recall that
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{so}(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\mathbf{q})\cong\mathfrak{so}(4,3)
is the subalgebra of derivations of
(\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}),\times)
. We will suppress the volume forms from the notation.

Definition 4.3
.

A
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle on
X
is an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\eta)
equipped with a holomorphic bundle map
\times:\Lambda^{2}(\mathcal{V}\oplus\mathcal{U})\to\mathcal{V}\oplus\mathcal{U}
such that

•

(\mathcal{V}_{p}\oplus\mathcal{U}_{p},\times_{p})\cong(\Im(\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{C}},\times)
for every
p\in X
, and

•

\Phi(s\times t)=\Phi(s)\times t+s\times\Phi(t)
, were
\Phi
is given by (
18
) and
s,t
are any sections of
\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V}
.

Remark 4.4
.

By Remark
2.7
, this implies that
\times|_{\Lambda^{2}\mathcal{U}}:\Lambda^{2}\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}
defines a holomorphic isomorphism between
\mathcal{V}
and the self-dual part
\Lambda^{2}_{+}\mathcal{U}
of the second exterior product.

Remark 4.5
.

Consider the flat connection
D
arising from an alternating holomorphic curve. Using the notation of Theorem
3.23

(\mathcal{E},\times,\Phi)
is a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle, where
\Phi
is the
(1,0)
-part of
D-\nabla
.

4.3.
An example

The Higgs bundles relevant to this paper have the additional property that the bundle will have a holomorphic reduction to the maximal torus. This means that the vector bundle decomposes as a direct sum of holomorphic line bundles. Specifically,

(19)

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3},

where each
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is holomorphic a line bundle. We furthermore impose
\mathcal{L}_{-i}=\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{O}
.
With respect to the splitting (
19
), a Higgs field
\Phi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{K}
can be written as a
7\times 7
-matrix whose entries are holomorphic sections of appropriate line bundles. We will consider Higgs fields of the form

(20)

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&\delta&0\\
\alpha&0&0&0&0&0&\delta\\
0&\beta&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&\gamma&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\gamma&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&\beta&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&\alpha&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{K},

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
are holomorphic sections of
\mathcal{L}_{3}\mathcal{L}_{-2}\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{-1}\mathcal{K},

\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{-2}\mathcal{K}
, respectively. To avoid writing
7\times 7
matrices, we will represent such an object by

(21)

(\mathcal{E},\Phi):=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.2253pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 142.47444pt\raise 29.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 53.14227pt\raise 4.27844pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since
\operatorname{tr}(\Phi)=0
and
\Lambda^{7}\mathcal{E}
is trivializable,
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
an
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle.

In fact, this example defines an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\eta)
, where

•

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}
and
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}
,

•

Q_{\mathcal{U}},~Q_{\mathcal{V}}
are orthogonal structures on
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
defined by

Q_{\mathcal{U}}=\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\
&\iddots&\\
1\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ Q_{\mathcal{V}}=\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\
&1\\
1\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{*},

•

\eta=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0&0&\delta\\
0&\gamma&0&0\\
0&0&\beta&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes K
.

For this example to be a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle, we need a holomorphic map

\times:\Lambda^{2}(\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V},

such that
(\mathcal{U}_{p}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{p},\times)\cong(\Im(\mathbb{H})_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{C}},\times)
for each
p\in X
. This implies
\mathcal{V}\cong\Lambda^{2}_{+}\mathcal{U}
. We will define a product as in the complex Frenet framing (
14
) of an alternating holomorphic curve.

Using
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{O}
, if
s_{0}=1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{L}_{0})
, then define

s_{0}\times(\cdot)=\mathrm{diag}(+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i},0,+\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}):\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}\to\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}.

If we set
\mathcal{L}_{-2}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{1},
then any nonzero complex number
\xi
determines an isomorphism

\xi:\mathcal{L}_{-3}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}\to\mathcal{L}_{-2}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{1}.

Using the isomorphism, we can define a product as in Lemma
3.21
.
Namely, given sections
s_{-3},s_{1}
of
\mathcal{L}_{-3}
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
:

s_{-3}\times s_{1}=\xi\cdot s_{-3}\otimes s_{1}.

The multiplication table is the same as that of
\{f_{-3},\dots,f_{3}\}
of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}
from (
7
) where the
\sqrt{2}
is replaced by
\xi
.

Lemma 4.6
.

With the above choice of product
\times:\Lambda^{2}(\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V}
, the Higgs bundles (
20
) is a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle if and only if we have the following equation in
H^{0}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{L}_{-3},\mathcal{L}_{-2}))

\alpha(\cdot)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma(s_{0})\times(\cdot),

where
s_{0}=1\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{L}_{0})
is a trivializing unit section.

Proof.

To be a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle, the Higgs field
\Phi
must be a derivation. We compute
\Phi(s_{0}\times s_{-3})=\Phi(s_{0})\times s_{-3}+s_{0}\times\Phi(s_{-3})
implies
\alpha(s_{-3})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma(s_{0})\times s_{-3}.
Indeed,

\Phi(s_{0})\times s_{-3}+s_{0}\times\Phi(s_{-3})=\gamma(s_{0})\times s_{-3}-\mathrm{i}\alpha(s_{-3}),

while
\Phi(s_{0}\times s_{-3})=\mathrm{i}\Phi(s_{-3})=\mathrm{i}\alpha(s_{-3})
. To see that this is the only condition on the Higgs field, one computes
\Phi(s_{a}\times s_{b})
and
\Phi(s_{a})\times s_{b}+s_{a}\times\Phi(b)
using the multiplication table (
7
), we leave this to the reader.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Note that if
\mathcal{L}_{-2}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\alpha
is a scalar multiple of
\gamma,
then we can define a product
\times
making a Higgs bundle of the form (
21
) a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle.

4.4.
Harmonic bundles

A
\mathsf{G}
-harmonic bundle is a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle equipped with a metric which solves a PDE related to the flatness of a connection.
We will focus on the vector bundle description.

Definition 4.8
.

An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundle on
X
is an
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
equipped with a hermitian metric
h
which induces the trivial metric on
\Lambda^{n}\mathcal{E}
and satisfies the Hitchin equations

(22)

F_{h}+[\Phi,\Phi^{*}]=0~,

where
F_{h}
is the curvature of the Chern connection
\nabla_{h}
of
h
and
\Phi^{*}
is the hermitian adjoint of
\Phi.

Let
(E,h)
be a smooth bundle unitary bundle. Two harmonic bundles
(\mathcal{E},\Phi,h)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},h)
on
(E,h)
are isomorphic if they differ by unitary bundle automorphism.

Given an
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi,h)
, the Hitchin equations (
22
) are equivalent to the flatness of the connection

D=\nabla_{h}+\Phi+\Phi^{*}~.

Hence an
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundle defines a (conjugacy class of) representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
such that
E\cong\widetilde{X}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Given a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
, a metric
h_{\rho}
on the flat bundle
\widetilde{X}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{C}^{n}
can be interpreted as a
\rho
-equivariant map to the Riemannian symmetric space:

h_{\rho}:\widetilde{X}\to\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})/\mathsf{SU}(n).

A metric
h_{\rho}
is called harmonic if, for every compact subset
K\subset\widetilde{X},
it is a critical point of the energy

\mathcal{E}_{K}(h_{\rho})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{K}|dh_{\rho}|^{2}.

This makes sense since, for two dimensional domains, the energy only depends on the conformal structure of the domain.
It turns out that a metric
h
solves the Hitchin equations (
22
) if and only if the
\rho
-equivariant map
h_{\rho}
is harmonic.

For
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles which have extra structures related to being a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle, the harmonic metric
h
is assumed to be compatible with these structures, i.e., the Chern connection
\nabla_{h}
has holonomy in the compact group
\mathsf{K}<\mathsf{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and hence the associated flat connection
D
has holonomy in
\mathsf{G}
. In terms of harmonic maps, the associated harmonic map factors through a copy of the symmetric space
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}\subset\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})/\mathsf{SU}(n).

For example, a hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{E}
is compatible with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear from
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
if there exists a conjugate linear involution
\lambda:\mathcal{E}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}
such that
h(e_{1},e_{2})=Q(e_{1},\lambda(e_{2}))
. In particular, viewing
h
as an isomorphism
H:\mathcal{E}\to\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
and
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
as an isomorphism
Q:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{*}
, we have
\lambda=Q^{-1}\circ H
; hence
\overline{H}\circ Q^{-1}\circ H=\overline{Q}.

An
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-harmonic metric on an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\eta)
consists of two hermitian metrics
h_{\mathcal{U}},h_{\mathcal{V}}
on
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}
which are compatible with
Q_{\mathcal{U}},Q_{\mathcal{V}}
, respectively. Note that the associated flat bundle
E
decomposes as
E=U\oplus V
, where
U\subset\mathcal{U}
and
V\subset\mathcal{V}
. Moreover,
Q_{\mathcal{U}}|_{U}\oplus(-Q_{\mathcal{V}})|_{V}
defines a parallel metric on
E
of signature
(4,3).

For an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle which is a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle, the quadratic form
Q_{\mathcal{V}}
is determined by
Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and the product
\times.
We have the following definition.

Definition 4.9
.

A
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic metric on a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{U},Q_{\mathcal{U}},\mathcal{V},Q_{\mathcal{V}},\times,\eta)
is a pair of compatible metrics
h_{\mathcal{U}},h_{\mathcal{V}}
on
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}
, respectively, such that the involutions
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}},\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}
satisfy

\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}(u_{1}\times u_{2})=\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{1})\times\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{2}).

Remark 4.10
.

Note that the flat bundle
E
associated to a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic bundle decomposes as
E=U\oplus V
and has a parallel product
\times:\Lambda^{2}E\to E
which identifies the fibers with
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}).

4.5.
Cyclic Higgs and harmonic bundles

The explicit Higgs bundles considered in Section
4.3
have additional symmetries which make them
6
-cyclic Higgs bundles.

Definition 4.11
.

An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is called
k
-cyclic if there is a holomorphic splitting
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that
\Phi(\mathcal{E}_{i})\subset\mathcal{E}_{i+1}\otimes\mathcal{K}
, where
i+1
is taken mod
k.
The splitting
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}
will be called the cyclic splitting.

Definition 4.12
.

An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi,h)
is called a
k
-cyclic harmonic bundle if
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is
k
-cyclic and the cyclic splitting is orthogonal with respect to the metric
h.

Two
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k},\Phi)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
are isomorphic if there is a holomorphic bundle automorphism which identifies
\mathcal{E}_{j}\cong\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j
and
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
. Similarly, two
k
-cyclic harmonic bundles are isomorphic if such an isomorphism is unitary. We note that cyclic Higgs bundles and cyclic harmonic bundles have a cyclic group symmetry. Namely, if
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is a
k
-cyclic Higgs bundle, consider the unitary holomorphic bundle automorphism

g=\mathrm{diag}(\zeta^{a}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}},\zeta^{a-1}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{E}_{2}},\dots,\zeta^{a-k+1}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}):\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}\to\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k},

where
\zeta
is a primitive
k^{th}
root of unity and
a
is chosen so that
\det(g)=1.
Then
g
acts on
\Phi
by

g\cdot\Phi=g^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ g=\zeta\cdot\Phi.

Remark 4.13
.

When
X
is compact, polystability of the above Higgs bundles automatically implies the existence of cyclic harmonic metrics, see Proposition
5.3
.
When
X
is noncompact, see
[
21
,
22
]
for results regarding the existence and uniqueness of cyclic harmonic metrics.

Lemma 4.14
.

The Higgs bundles from (
21
) are
6
-cyclic with cyclic splitting

\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{3}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{5}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{6}=(\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3})\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}.

Moreover, if
h
is a cyclic
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-harmonic metric, then
h=h_{-3}\oplus h_{-2}\oplus\cdots\oplus h_{2}\oplus h_{3}
where
h_{a}
is a hermitian metric on
\mathcal{L}_{a}
and
h_{-a}=h_{a}^{-1}.

Proof.

The Higgs bundle is
6
-cyclic since rearranging the summands from (
21
) yields

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.97227pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.97227pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 194.03857pt\raise-12.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 211.30986pt\raise-3.91597pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 211.30577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.38454pt\raise 31.09224pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.34224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\delta\ \ \ \alpha)^{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 18.28835pt\raise 7.32683pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Recall that this is
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-Higgs bundle, with
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}
,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}
and quadratic forms

(23)

Q_{\mathcal{U}}=\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\
&\iddots\\
1\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ Q_{\mathcal{V}}=\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\
&1\\
1\end{pmatrix}~,

Thus, an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-cyclic harmonic metric
h_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus\ h_{\mathcal{V}}
is diagonal in the cyclic splitting. The condition that
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}=Q_{\mathcal{U}}^{-1}\circ h_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=Q_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}\circ h_{\mathcal{V}}
are conjugate linear involutions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
, respectively, implies that the metric on
\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}
splits as
h_{-3}\oplus h_{3}
, where
h_{3}^{-1}=h_{-3}
, and that the metric
h_{j}
on
\mathcal{L}_{j}
satisfies
h_{j}^{-1}=h_{-j}.

∎

Lemma 4.15
.

When
\mathcal{L}_{-2}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\alpha
is a scalar multiple of
\gamma
, a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic metric is an
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
-cyclic harmonic metric where the metric
h_{-2}
on
\mathcal{L}_{-2}
is
h_{-3}h_{1}
and the metric
h_{2}
on
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is
h_{3}h_{-1}
.

Proof.

Recall from Remark
4.7
that when
\mathcal{L}_{-2}=\mathcal{L}_{-3}\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\alpha
and
\gamma
are scalar multiples of each other, the Higgs bundle under consideration is a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle.
Thus, the conjugate linear involutions
\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}=Q_{\mathcal{U}}^{-1}\circ h_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}=Q_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}\circ h_{\mathcal{V}}
associated to a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic metric satisfies

\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{1})\times\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(u_{2})=\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}(u_{1}\times u_{2}),

for all
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{U}.

If
\alpha=\frac{\mathrm{i}\xi}{2}\gamma
, then
s_{-3}\times s_{1}=\xi\cdot s_{-3}\otimes s_{1}.
Using the metric decomposition from Lemma
4.14
, we have

\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(s_{-3})\times\lambda_{\mathcal{U}}(s_{1})=h_{-3}h_{1}s_{3}\times s_{-1}=h_{-3}h_{1}\xi(s_{3}\otimes s_{-1}),

\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}(s_{-3}\times s_{1})=\lambda_{\mathcal{V}}(\xi(s_{-3}\otimes s_{1}))=h_{-2}\xi(s_{3}\otimes s_{1}).

Hence,
h_{-2}=h_{-3}h_{1}
and
h_{2}=h_{3}h_{-1}.

∎

We now deduce a decomposition of the flat bundle associated to these
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles which is analogous to
[
7
, Theorem 2.33]
.
Let
D=\nabla_{h}+\Phi+\Phi^{*}
be the associated flat connection. Since the metric is diagonal, in the splitting
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3},
the connection
D
decomposes as in (
15
) of Theorem
3.23
where
\mathcal{T}
is replaced with
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{B}
is replaced with
\mathcal{L}_{-3}
, and
1
and
1^{*}
are replaced with
\alpha
and
\alpha^{*}.

The associated
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-flat bundle
E_{D}\subset\mathcal{E}
is the fixed point locus of the antilinear involution
\lambda:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}
defined by
h(u,v)=Q(u,\lambda(v))
.
The bundle
E_{D}
decomposes as
E_{D}=U\oplus V
, where
U\subset\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}
and
V\subset\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}.

Proposition 4.16
.

The flat bundle
E_{D}
of a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundle of the form (
21
) decomposes as

E_{D}=\ell\oplus U_{1}\oplus V_{1}\oplus U_{2},

where
\ell\subset\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
V_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2}
are negative definite subbundles of rank 1 and 2 respectively, and
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
U_{2}\subset\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3}
are positive definite rank 2 subbundles.

Proof.

The complex bilinear forms
Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and
Q_{\mathcal{V}}
are given by (
23
). Since the metric
h
is diagonal, in the splitting
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus(\mathcal{L}_{-1}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1})\oplus(\mathcal{L}_{-2}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{2})\oplus(\mathcal{L}_{-3}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{3})
, the involution
\lambda
is given by

\lambda=-h_{0}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}&h_{1}\\
h_{1}^{-1}&\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}&-h_{2}\\
-h_{2}^{-1}&\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}&h_{3}\\
h_{3}^{-1}&\end{pmatrix},

where
h_{j}(w):\mathcal{L}_{j}\to\mathcal{L}_{-j}
is the antilinear map defined by
h_{j}(w)(w^{\prime})=h_{j}(w,w^{\prime}).

∎

4.6.
Cyclic harmonic bundles and equivariant alternating holomorphic maps

We now establish a 1-1 correspondence between isomorphism classes of certain
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles and equivariant alternating holomorphic curves.
Fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in S
and let
\pi_{1}(S)=\pi_{1}(S,x_{0})
denote the fundamental group of
S
. Fix also a universal covering
\widetilde{S}\to S
and a lift
\tilde{x}_{0}
of the basepoint. We will suppress the basepoints from the notation.

Definition 4.17
.

An equivariant alternating holomorphic curve on
S
is a pair
(\rho,f)
consisting of a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and an alternating holomorphic curve
f:\widetilde{S}\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which is
\rho
-equivariant, that is,
f(\gamma\cdot x)=\rho(\gamma)\cdot f(x)
for all
x\in\widetilde{S}
and all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S).

When
S
is simply connected an alternating holomorphic curve is of course the same as an equivariant alternating holomorphic curve. For example,
(S,\mathsf{j})
could be isomorphic to the complex plane as in
[
13
]
. Note that the Riemann surface structure
\widetilde{X}=(\widetilde{S},\mathsf{j})
induced by an equivariant alternating holomorphic curve
(\rho,f)
descends to a Riemann surface structure
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
.

Definition 4.18
.

Two equivariant alternating holomorphic curves
(\rho_{1},f_{1})
and
(\rho_{2},f_{2})
are isomorphic if there exists
g\in\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\psi\in\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(S)
such that

(\rho_{1},f_{1})=(\mathrm{Ad}_{g}\circ\rho_{2},(g\cdot f)\circ\tilde{\psi}),

where
\tilde{\psi}
is the pullback of
\psi
to
\widetilde{S}.

Remark 4.19
.

Since
\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(S)
acts freely on the space of complex structures on
S
, two equivariant holomorphic curves
(\rho_{1},f_{1})
and
(\rho_{2},f_{2})
which induce the same Riemann surface
X
are isomorphic if and only if there is
g\in\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
such that

(\rho_{1},f_{1})=(\mathrm{Ad}_{g}\circ\rho_{2},g\cdot f_{2}).

For the discussion below, let
\mathcal{B}
be a holomorphic line bundle on
X
, and consider a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundle
(\mathcal{E},\times,\Phi,h)
of the form (
21
) with
\mathcal{L}_{-3}=\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{L}_{-1}=\mathcal{K},
and assume
\beta
is nonzero. The Higgs bundle is written schematically as

(24)

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 187.60104pt\raise-12.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 203.09874pt\raise-4.93213pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 203.0974pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
