0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 234.1391pt\raise-10.6994pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6994pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 255.13742pt\raise-3.98785pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.74178pt\raise 29.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 6.51996pt\raise 2.79625pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 255.13914pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.09947pt\raise 29.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 58.5724pt\raise 5.70694pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Here the map
1:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is the identity and
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is determined by the product
\times.
Namely, for a section
b
of
\mathcal{B}
and
s_{0}=1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O})
, we have

-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(b)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\cdot 1(s_{0})\times b~.

Proposition 4.20
.

Two
\mathsf{G}_{2}
-cyclic harmonic bundles of the form (
24
) determined by
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)
and
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
are isomorphic if and only if
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)=(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\lambda\beta,\lambda^{-2}\delta)
for some
\lambda\in\mathsf{U}(1).

Proof.

Such an isomorphism is given by a diagonal unitary gauge transformation
g
which has
\det(g)=1,
preserves the orthogonal structures
Q_{\mathcal{U}}
and
Q_{\mathcal{V}}
and the product. As a result

g=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda,\lambda\mu^{-1},\mu^{-1},1,\mu,\lambda^{-1}\mu,\lambda^{-1}),

where
\lambda,\mu\in\mathsf{U}(1).
Such a gauge transformation preserves the map
1:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{O}\otimes\mathcal{K}
if and only if
\mu=1.

Hence, such a gauge transformation acts on
(\beta,\delta)
by
\beta\mapsto\lambda^{-1}\beta
and
\delta\mapsto\lambda^{2}\delta.

∎

Theorem 4.21
.

There is a 1-1 correspondence between isomorphism classes of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves with induced Riemann surface
X
and isomorphism classes of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles of the form (
24
) on
X
.

Proof.

One direction essentially follows from the description of the complex Frenet framing in Theorem
3.23
.
As in §
3.6
, let
(E,\times,D)
be the pullback of the trivial
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
-bundle and connection on
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
by
f
to
\widetilde{X}.
Recall the Frenet framing of
f
induces decomposition
E=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathrm{T}S\oplus\mathrm{N}S\oplus\mathrm{B}S
which is orthogonal with respect to a positive definite metric
\mathbf{g}=1\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{T}}\oplus(-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{N}})\oplus\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{B}}
.
By Theorem
3.23
, the complex Frenet framing of
f
defines a holomorphic structure
\mathcal{E}
on
E_{\mathbb{C}}
which decomposes as

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1},

where we have written
\mathcal{K}^{-1}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}
.
This splitting is orthogonal with respect to the induced hermitian metric
h
, and
\Phi=(D-\nabla_{h})^{1,0}
is holomorphic. In particular,
(\mathcal{E},\times,\Phi,h)
defines a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundle. Rearranging the summands of the splitting, the cyclic Higgs bundle on
\widetilde{X}
has the desired form. Everything descends to
X
equivariance.
By (
16
) and Proposition
4.20
, two equivariant holomorphic curves which induce the Riemann surface
X
are isomorphic if and only if the associated
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles are isomorphic.

We now construct an isomorphism class of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves from a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundle of the form (
24
). By Proposition
4.16
, the bundle
E_{D}\subset\mathcal{E}
decomposes as
\ell\oplus U_{1}\oplus V_{1}\oplus U_{2}.
Where
\ell\subset\mathcal{O}
is a negative definite line subbundle. Moreover,
\ell
is the span of the section
s_{0}=1:X\to\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{E}
, and so
s_{0}(x)
is a vector in
E_{D}
of norm
-1.

The pullback
\widetilde{E}_{D}
of
E_{D}
to the universal covering is trivialized by parallel transport by
D.
In this trivialization, the pullback of
s_{0}
defines a
\rho
-equivariant map to the space of norm
-1
-vectors in the fiber over the base point
\widetilde{E}_{\tilde{x}_{0}}
.
Choosing an identification of
(\widetilde{E}_{\tilde{x}_{0}},\times)\cong\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
defines an equivariant curve
f:\widetilde{X}\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}.
By construction, the complex Frenet framing of
f
the decomposition of the flat connection
D
in the splitting
\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}.
In particular,
f
is an alternating holomorphic curve. Changing the identification with
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
gives isomorphic equivariant alternating holomorphic curves.
∎

5.
Moduli spaces for compact surfaces

Fix a closed surface
\Sigma
with genus
g\geq 2
, and let
X
denote a Riemann surface structure on
\Sigma.
In this section we prove Theorem
5.4
which gives a holomorphic description of the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles which arise from equivariant holomorphic curves on
\Sigma
with induced Riemann surface
X
. In particular, the associated moduli space has many connected components.
Then, in Theorem
5.9
, we describe the moduli space of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves on
\Sigma
, where the induced Riemann surface is allowed to vary, as a complex analytic space with a surjective holomorphic map to the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
and with a holomorphic action of the mapping class group; here the fibers are described by Theorem
5.4
.
Finally, we describe how some of the connected components correspond to Hitchin representations for the groups
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
, and discuss the totally geodesic case in §
5.7
.
We start by recalling the moduli space of Higgs bundles on a compact Riemann surface and the nonabelian Hodge correspondence.

5.1.
Moduli spaces for fixed Riemann surface

To form the moduli space of Higgs bundles on
X
, we need the notion of stability. An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
on
X
is called

•

semistable
if, for every proper holomorphic subbundle
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{E}
such that
\Phi(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}
, we have
\text{deg}(\mathcal{F})\leq 0,

•

stable
if it is the above inequality is always strict, and

•

polystable
if it is a direct sum of stable Higgs bundles of degree
0
, that is,
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}
and, for all
i
,
\text{deg}(\mathcal{E}_{i})=0
,
\Phi(\mathcal{E}_{i})\subset\mathcal{E}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{K}
and
(\mathcal{E}_{i},\Phi|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}})
is a stable Higgs bundle.

The following theorem was proven by Nitsure
[
28
]
and Simpson
[
33
]
.

Theorem 5.1
.

There is a quasi-projective variety
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C}))
, called the moduli space of
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles, whose points parametrize isomorphism classes of polystable
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles on a compact Riemann
X
.

There are appropriate notions of stability for
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles and associated moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G})
, see
[
33
,
15
]
. When
\mathsf{G}
is complex reductive, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G})
is quasi-projective.

Since (poly)stability is preserved by scaling the Higgs field by
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, there is a natural algebraic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G})
. The fixed point set of any subgroup of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
thus defines an algebraic subvariety.

The following theorem relates the notion of stability to the existence of a harmonic metric. It was proven by Hitchin
[
16
]
for
n=2
and Simpson
[
32
]
in general.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 2.
An
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
on
X
is polystable if and only if there is a hermitian metric
h
on
\mathcal{E}
solving the Hitchin equations (
22
). In particular,
(\mathcal{E},\Phi,h)
is an
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundle and
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-harmonic bundles define polystable Higgs bundles.

For
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles on compact Riemann surfaces, a similar theorem holds. Namely, if
(\mathcal{P},\Phi)
is a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle, then it is polystable if and only if there is a metric
h
such that
(\mathcal{P},\Phi,h)
is a
\mathsf{G}
-harmonic bundle. This correspondence is usually called the
Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence
. For
\mathsf{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles which have extra structures related to being a
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundle, the metric solving the Hitchin equations is compatible with these structures, see
[
15
]
for details.

For
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles on compact Riemann surfaces, we have the following result of Simpson which implies that polystable
k
-cyclic Higgs bundles on a compact Riemann surface are automatically
k
-cyclic harmonic bundles.

Proposition 5.3
.

[
31
]

Let
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
be a
k
-cyclic Higgs bundle with
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Then
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is stable if and only if for all proper holomorphic subbundles
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{E}
with
\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\Phi(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}
, we have
\text{deg}(\mathcal{F})<0.
Moreover, when
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is stable, the splitting
\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{k}
is orthogonal with respect to the hermitian metric
h
solving the Hitchin equations (
22
).

A representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}
is called
reductive
if post composing
\rho
with the adjoint representation of
\mathsf{G}
decomposes as a direct sum of irreducible representations. The moduli space of
\mathsf{G}
-conjugacy classes of representations
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}
is called the
character variety
and denoted by

\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G})=\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathrm{red}}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathsf{G})/\mathsf{G}~.

Corlette’s theorem
[
9
]
(proven by Donaldson
[
10
]
for
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
) asserts that given a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}
, there exists a
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
h_{\rho}:\widetilde{X}\to\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{K}
if and only if
\rho
is reductive.
Combining Corlette’s theorem with the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence defines a real analytic isomorphism between the moduli space of
\mathsf{G}
-Higgs bundles on
X
, the moduli space of
\mathsf{G}
-harmonic bundles on
X
and the
\mathsf{G}
-character variety. This is called the
nonabelian Hodge correspondence
.

5.2.
Moduli spaces of holomorphic curves for compact Riemann surfaces

For a compact Riemann surface
X,
denote the set of isomorphism classes of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves
(\rho,f)
with induced Riemann surface
X
by

\mathcal{H}(X)=\{(\rho,f)~\text{equiv. alt. holomorphic curves with induced complex structure }X\}/\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}.

By the nonabelian Hodge correspondence and Theorem
4.21
,
\mathcal{H}(X)
is in 1-1 correspondence with the isomorphism classes of polystable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles on
X
of the form

(25)

0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.74178pt\raise 29.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 6.51996pt\raise 2.79625pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 255.13914pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.09947pt\raise 29.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 58.5724pt\raise 5.70694pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\mathcal{B}
is a holomorphic line bundle,
\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\},

1:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is the identity, and
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is defined by
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(b)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\cdot 1(s_{0})\times b
, where
s_{0}=1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O}).

This correspondence gives
\mathcal{H}(X)
a complex analytic structure.

The degree of the line bundle
\mathcal{B}
defines a continuous map
\text{deg}:\mathcal{H}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,

\mathcal{H}(X)=\coprod\limits_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{d}(X),

where
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)=\text{deg}^{-1}(d).
The spaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
parameterized as follows.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 2
.

(1)

If
g\leq d\leq 6g-6
, then
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
is biholomorphic to a rank
(2d-g+1)
holomorphic vector bundle over the
(6g-6-d)
-symmetric product of
X
.

(2)

If
0\leq d\leq g-1,
then
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
is biholomorphic to bundle over a
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-cover of the
2d
-symmetric product of
X
whose fiber is
(\mathbb{C}^{5g-5-d}\setminus\{0\})/\pm\mathrm{Id}
.

(3)

If
d\notin\{0,\dots,6g-6\}
, then
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
is empty.

Remark 5.5
.

The upper bound on
d
follows immediately from the assumption that
\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
while the lower bound comes from polystability. To prove first two points, we parameterize the isomorphism classes of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles of the form (
25
) which are polystable. The proof gives explicit descriptions of the fibrations over the appropriate symmetric product.

The following proposition describes when the associated
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is polystable.

Proposition 5.6
.

For a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle of the form (
25
), let
d=\text{deg}(\mathcal{B}).
Then
0\leq d\leq 6g-6
, and,

•

for
g\leq d\leq 6g-6
, the associated
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is stable,

•

for
0<d\leq g-1
, the associated
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is polystable if and only if
\delta\neq 0
, in which case it is stable,

•

for
d=0
, the associated
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is polystable if and only if
\delta\neq 0,
in which case it is strictly polystable.

Proof.

Recall that the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundles are
6
-cyclic with cyclic splitting

(\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1})\oplus\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}.

By Proposition
5.3
, to test stability of the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle it suffices to consider invariant subbbundles compatible with the cyclic splitting.

When
\delta=0,

\mathcal{B}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}
is such an invariant bundle. If
d<g-1
, then the degree of
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}
is positive and invariant so the underlying Higgs bundle is unstable, while for
d=g-1
,
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}
is a degree zero invariant subbundle which does not have an invariant complement, so the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is not polystable. Hence, the Higgs bundles with
\delta=0
are polystable if and only if
d>g-1
, in which case they are stable.

When
\delta\neq 0
, the only invariant subbundle is the degree
-d
kernel of

(26)

\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\\
\delta\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\to(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1})\otimes\mathcal{K}~.

Hence, when
0<d\leq 6g-6
and
\delta\neq 0
, the associated
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is stable.

Finally, when
d=0
,
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})\setminus\{0\}
defines an isomorphism
\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{B}^{-1}.
Denote the kernel of (
26
) by
\mathcal{I}
. Since
\delta\neq 0,

\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}
is an orthogonal subbundle which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}
. Hence, taking the orthogonal complement, we obtain a new splitting
\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}\cong\mathcal{I}\oplus\mathcal{I}^{\perp}
.
In this splitting the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is given by
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)=(\mathcal{I}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\prime},0\oplus\Phi^{\prime})
, where
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is the
6
-cyclic Higgs bundle

(27)

(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.02087pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.02087pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 38.02425pt\raise-3.11552pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise with
\epsilon
is the restriction of (
26
) to
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}.

Since
\epsilon\neq 0
, the cyclic Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is stable, and hence
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is strictly polystable.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Note that in the case
0\leq d\leq g-1,
the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle associated to the above
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle is polystable when
\beta=0
. Such Higgs bundles define equivariant holomorphic curve
f:\tilde{X}\to\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
which are totally geodesic.

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Denote the set of degree
n
line bundles on
X
by
\mathsf{Pic}^{n}(X)
and the
n^{th}
symmetric product by
\mathsf{Sym}^{n}(X).
Taking the divisor
\mathrm{div}(\sigma)
defines a biholomorphism between
\mathsf{Sym}^{n}(X)
and the space of pairs
(\mathcal{N},[\sigma])
, where
\mathcal{N}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{n}(X)
and
[\sigma]\in\mathbf{P}(H^{0}(\mathcal{N}))
.

First consider the case when
d=\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})
satisfies
g\leq d\leq 6g-6
. The proof is similar to Hitchin’s parameterization of the components of
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
with nonzero Euler number in
[
16
]
. By Proposition
4.20
and Proposition
5.6
, we have

\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)=\{(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)\}/\sim~,

where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{d}(X),

\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
and
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})
, and
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)\sim(\mathcal{B},\lambda\beta,\lambda^{-2}\delta)
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}.
Define the bundle
\pi:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathsf{Sym}^{6g-6-d}(X)
by
\pi^{-1}(D)=H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6}(-2D))
. Since
d\geq g
, Riemann-Roch and Serre duality imply
\dim(\pi^{-1}(D))=2d-(g-1)
.
Hence,
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathsf{Sym}^{6g-6-d}(X)
is a rank
2d-(g-1)
vector bundle.
There is a well defined injective map

\Psi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.70313pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.50505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.88025pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.88025pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-22.70313pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.70313pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.70313pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.70313pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left(\mathrm{div}(\beta),\beta^{2}\delta\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

Moreover, the map
\Psi
is surjective. Indeed, a point
(D,\theta)\in\mathcal{Y}
determines
(\mathcal{L},[\beta])
, where
\beta\in\mathbf{P}(H^{0}(\mathcal{L}))
with
\mathrm{div}(\beta)=D
and
\theta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6}(-2D)).
Consider
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{d}(X)
, since
\theta
is degree six holomorphic differential which vanishes on
2D
, it can be written as
\theta=\beta^{2}\delta
for
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2}).

The result follows since holomorphic bijections are biholomorphisms.

Now assume
0\leq d\leq g-1.
By Proposition
4.20
and Proposition
5.6
, we have

\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)=\{(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)\}/\sim~,

where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{d}(X),

\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
,
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})\setminus\{0\}
, and
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)\sim(\mathcal{B},\lambda\beta,\lambda^{-2}\delta)
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}.
Note that
\dim(H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})=5g-5-d.

Recall that the set of square roots of an even degree line bundle over
X
is a
H^{1}(S,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-torsor. The
2d
-symmetric product
\mathsf{Sym}^{2d}(X)
thus has a
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
-cover
\widehat{\mathsf{Sym}}^{2d}(X)
which parameterizes pairs
(\mathcal{B},[\delta])
, where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{d}(X)
and
[\delta]\in\mathbf{P}(H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})\setminus\{0\}).

There is a well defined injective map

\Theta:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.70313pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.50505pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.70313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.70313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widehat{\mathsf{Sym}}^{2d}(X)\times H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6})}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-22.70313pt\raise-8.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.70313pt\raise-8.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.10919pt\raise-8.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.10919pt\raise-8.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left((\mathcal{B},[\delta]),\beta^{2}\delta\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Let
\mathcal{W}
denote the image of
\Theta
. Projection on to the first factor defines a surjective map
\pi:\mathcal{W}\to\widehat{\mathsf{Sym}}^{2d}(X).
The fiber
\pi^{-1}((\mathcal{B},[\delta]))
consists of all nonzero holomorphic sections of
\mathcal{K}^{6}
which are squares of sections
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3}
and vanish on
\mathrm{div}(\delta)
. In other words, the fiber is the image of the quadratic map
H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}\to H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-2}\mathcal{K}^{6})\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6})
defined by
\beta\mapsto\beta^{2}.
In particular, it is biholomorphic to
(\mathbb{C}^{5g-5-d}\setminus\{0\})/\pm\mathrm{Id}.

∎

Remark 5.8
.

By Remark
5.7
, when
0\leq d\leq g-1
and
\beta=0
, the resulting Higgs bundles are also polystable. By the above proof, the space of such Higgs bundles is biholomorphic the quotient of a rank
(5g-5-d)
vector bundle over
\widehat{\mathsf{Sym}}^{2d}(X)
by the fiberwise action of
\pm\mathrm{Id}.

5.3.
Varying the Riemann surface

The moduli space
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
of equivariant alternating holomorphic curves on
\Sigma
is defined by

\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)=\{(\rho,f)~\text{equivariant alternating holomorphic curve }\}/\sim~,

where
(f_{1},\rho_{1})\sim(f_{2},\rho_{2})
if there is
g\in\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
and
f\in\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
such that
(\rho_{1},f_{1})=(\mathrm{Ad}_{g}\circ\rho_{2},g\cdot f_{2}).

Recall that the
Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
is the quotient of the space of complex structures on
\Sigma
which are compatible with the orientation by the group
\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma).

Taking the induced Riemann surface of an equivariant holomorphic curve defines a surjective map

\pi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.84898pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.25002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.84898pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.84898pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)~.}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-17.84898pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[(\rho,f)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.849pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.54868pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.54868pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[(S,\mathsf{j})]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Recall also that the
mapping class group

\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
is the quotient
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)=\mathsf{Diff}^{+}(\Sigma)/\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma).
The mapping class group acts naturally on
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
, and the map
\pi
is equivariant with respect to these actions.

Finally, recall that a
complex analytic set
is a subset
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
which is locally the zero set of a finite number of holomorphic functions. A holomorphic function on an open set
U\subset V
is the restriction of a holomorphic function defined on a neighborhood of
U
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. This defines a structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{V}
on
V
and we call a
complex analytic space
a ringed space
(A,\mathcal{O}_{A})
which is locally isomorphic to an analytic set equipped with its structure sheaf. A morphism of ringed space between complex analytic space is called a
holomorphic map
.

Theorem 5.9
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
has the structure of a complex analytic space. With respect to this structure, the mapping class group
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
acts holomorphically and the projection to Teichmüller space
\pi:\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is a surjective holomorphic map.
Moreover,

\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)=\coprod_{d\in\{0,\dots,6g-6\}}\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)~,

where, for each
X\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
,
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)\cap\pi^{-1}(X)
is biholomorphic to the space
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
from Theorem
5.4
.

Proof.

Recall now that from Section
4
provides a one-to-one correspondence between points in
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
and pair of points
(X,(\mathcal{E},\Phi))
where
X\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
and
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)\in\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
is a stable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
-Higgs bundles of the form of equation (
24
).

Consider the space

\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})=\coprod_{X\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)}\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})~,

where
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
is the moduli space of poly-stable
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
-Higgs bundles over
X
. Since
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
is a complex reductive algebraic group
\mathsf{G}
, building on the construction of Simpson
[
34
]
, it was proven in
[
2
, Theorem 7.5]
that
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
has a
\mathsf{Mod}(\Sigma)
-invariant complex analytic structure such that the natural projection map
\pi:\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is holomorphic and
\pi^{-1}(X)
is biholomorphic to
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
.

The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on each
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
yields a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
action on
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
which is analytic by
[
34
]
. Given a primitive
6^{th}
root of unity
\zeta
, the set of fixed points
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})^{\zeta}
is thus analytic.
To conclude the proof, observe that being of the form (
24
) is an open condition in the space of
6
-cyclic
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
-Higgs bundles. Since open subsets of analytic spaces are analytic, the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Simpson explained to us a proof that the restriction of the projection
\pi:\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
to stable Higgs bundles is a flat family. This implies in particular that the total space
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
, and hence
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
, is smooth. Since the technical aspects of the proof are not inline with the main points of this paper, we decided not to include it here. Smoothness will be explicitly addressed in
[
8
]
.

5.4.
The case
d=6g-6

Recall that a
Fuchsian representation
is a morphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
arising as the holonomy of a hyperbolic structure on
\Sigma
(compatible with the orientation). The space of Fuchsian representations defines a connected component of the character variety
\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
which is naturally identified with the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma).

For
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
a complex semisimple Lie group of adjoint type, there is a special embedding
\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
called the principal embedding. The restriction of the principal embedding to
\mathsf{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defines an embedding into the split real form
\mathsf{G}
of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Hence, postcomposing Fuchsian representations with the principal embedding define representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}
.
In
[
17
]
, Hitchin used Higgs bundles on a fixed Riemann surface to parameterize the connected component
\mathrm{Hit}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G})\subset\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G})
containing such representations by a vector space of holomorphic differentials. As a result,
\mathrm{Hit}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G})
is called the
Hitchin component
and representations in it are called
\mathsf{G}
-
Hitchin representations
.

Denote the vector bundle of degree
6
holomorphic differentials by
p:\mathcal{Q}_{6}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
, i.e,
p^{-1}(X)\cong H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6}_{X}).
For the case
d=6g-6,
we have the following description of
\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(\Sigma).

Theorem 5.11
.

The space
\pi:\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is biholomorphic to the vector bundle
p:\mathcal{Q}_{6}\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
. Moreover, the points of
\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(\Sigma)
corresponds to isomorphism classes of alternating holomorphic curves
(\rho,f)
with
\rho
a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representation.

Proof.

For a fixed Riemann surface
X
, the space
\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(X)
is parameterized by isomorphism classes of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles of the form (
25
) with
\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})=6g-6.

Such Higgs bundles are determined by triple
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)
, where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{6g-6}(X)
,
\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
and
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2}).
Since
\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})=6g-6
and
\beta\neq 0
, we have
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{K}^{3}
, and hence
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6}).
Furthermore, by Theorem
5.4
, fixing
\beta=1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})
determines the isomorphism class of such a Higgs bundles. Under the nonabelian Hodge correspondence, such Higgs bundles define
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representations, see
[
17
]
. We conclude the proof by noting that the fiber of
\pi:\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is defined by the holomorphic section
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{6})
by Theorem
5.4
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

In
[
19
]
, Labourie proved that the natural map
\mathcal{H}_{6g-6}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{Hit}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
is a diffeomorphism. A dimension count implies that
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
maps onto a connected component of
\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
if and only if
d=6g-6
. In §
6
we will discuss the local structure of the map
\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
for general
d
.

5.5.
The case
d=0

Recall that elements
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
correspond to representations
\rho_{\omega}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and also to holomorphic line bundles
\mathcal{I}_{\omega}
on a Riemann surface such that
\mathcal{I}_{\omega}^{2}=\mathcal{O}
.
Given
\omega
and a Riemann surface
X,
an
\omega
-twisted holomorphic cubic differential
is a holomorphic section of
\mathcal{K}_{X}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega}
. We denote the vector bundle of
\omega
-twisted holomorphic cubic differentials over Teichmüller space by
\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\omega}
.
For the case
d=0,
we have the following description of
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma).

Lemma 5.13
.

The space
\pi:\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
decomposes as

\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma)=\coprod_{\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma),

where
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma)
is biholomorphic to the quotient of the complement of the zero section of
\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\omega}
by
\pm\mathrm{Id}
.

Proof.

For a fixed Riemann surface
X
, the space
\mathcal{H}_{0}(X)
is parameterized by isomorphism classes of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles of the form (
25
) with
\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})=0.

Such Higgs bundles are determined by triple
(\mathcal{B},\beta,\delta)
, where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathsf{Pic}^{0}(X)
,
\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
and
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})\setminus\{0\}.
Since
\delta\neq 0,

\mathcal{B}^{2}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Hence
H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
parameterizes the choices for
\mathcal{B}
, and this gives the decomposition into components
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma)
. Note that
\beta
is an
\omega
-twisted holomorphic cubic differential since
\beta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{3})
and
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\cong\mathcal{B}.

By the proof of Theorem
5.4
, fixing
\delta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B}^{2})
determines the isomorphism class of the Higgs bundle up to
\beta\mapsto-\beta
.
Hence,
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma)
is the biholomorphic to the quotient of the complement of the zero section of
\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{\omega}
by
\pm\mathrm{Id}.

∎

We now describe the equivariant holomorphic curves in
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma)
in more detail.
Recall from §
3.3
that
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
is the space of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{4,3}
of norm 1 and
\mathbf{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{4,3})
is the space of positive definite lines in
\mathbb{R}^{4,3}
. The group
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
acts transitively on both
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
and
\mathbf{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{4,3})
, and the stabilizers of a point are respectively isomorphic to
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and the nonsplit extension

1\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 1~.

Since taking orthogonal complements defines a bijection between points in
\mathbf{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{4,3})
and totally geodesic copies of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
, the
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-stabilizer of an
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
is isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})
.
Finally, note that given a representation
\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})
, we get

•

an induced
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-representation
\rho_{\omega}=\varphi\circ\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and

•

an
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-representation
\hat{\rho}:\ker(\rho_{\omega})\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R}).

Theorem 5.14
.

For
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{2}),
the isomorphism class of an equivariant alternating holomorphic curve
[\rho,f]
lies in
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\omega}(\Sigma)
if and only if
f(\widetilde{\Sigma})
is contained in a unique totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
inside
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and

\rho=\iota\circ\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}~,

where

\iota:\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is given by the stabilizer of the unique
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
containing
f(\widetilde{\Sigma})
, and

\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})
has the property that the induced
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-representation is
\rho_{\omega}
and the associated
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
representation
\hat{\rho}:\ker(\rho_{\omega})\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
is an
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin representation which is not Fuchsian.

Remark 5.15
.

Note that an equivariant holomorphic curve
[\rho,f]
lies in a totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
if and only if the representation fixes a positive definite line in
\mathbb{R}^{4,3}
, and that this is equivalent to
\rho
factoring through the stabilizer of a positive definite line.

Proof.

First suppose
[\rho,f]\in\mathcal{H}^{\omega}_{0}
. Let
X
be the induced Riemann surface and
\mathcal{I}
the holomorphic line bundle on
X
associated to
\omega.

As in the proof of Proposition
5.6
, the
\mathsf{SL}(7,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle associated to
[\rho,f]
is strictly polystable and isomorphic to
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)\cong(\mathcal{I}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\prime},0\oplus\Phi^{\prime}),
where
\mathcal{I}\cong\mathcal{B}
is an orthogonal subbundle of
\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{-1}
defined by the kernel of (
26
) and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is given by (
27
).
Note that
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
defines an
\mathsf{O}(3,3)
-Higgs bundle, and that
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
is defines an
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{O}(1)\times\mathsf{O}(3,3))
-Higgs bundle.
This implies the
\mathsf{SO}_{0}(4,3)
representations
\rho
associated via the nonabelian Hodge correspondence fixes a positive definite line in
\mathbb{R}^{4,3}.

Hence, the image of
f
lies in the totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
associated to the positive definite line bundle
\mathcal{I}
and
\rho
factors through the stabilizer of a positive definite line in
\mathbb{R}^{4,3}.

Since
\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{I},
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-factor of the representation
\rho
is given by the
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
determined by
\mathcal{B}.

As
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}
are both isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}
, the Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
from (
27
) can be written as

(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 179.25014pt\raise-11.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 198.18668pt\raise-2.6819pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 198.18764pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 96.50356pt\raise 29.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 12.29065pt\raise 5.46057pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

First consider the case
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{O}.
In this case the
\rho_{\omega}
is the trivial representation and
\hat{\rho}
is an
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-representation.
Consider the following injective holomorphic bundle maps

\theta_{\pm}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.03479pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.03479pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.62965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 89.62965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-19.3669pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(x,y,z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.3669pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.03479pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.03479pt\raise-8.32pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\pm z,x,y,z,\pm x,\pm y)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

The images of
\theta_{\pm}
define holomorphic subbundles
\mathcal{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}\subset\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{+}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{-}
.
The bundles
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
are both
\Phi^{\prime}
-invariant since

\Phi(\theta_{\pm}(x,y,z))=\Phi(\pm z,x,y,z,\pm x,\pm y)=(\pm(1),\pm\beta(z),1(x),1(y),\beta(z),\pm 1(x))

=\theta_{\pm}(\phi_{\pm}(x,y,z))~,

where

\phi_{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\pm\beta\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathsf{End}(\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{-1})\otimes\mathcal{K}.

Moreover,
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},\phi_{\pm})
is the
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Higgs bundles in the Hitchin component described by the cubic differential
\pm\beta,
see
[
17
]
.
Hence, when
\omega=0,
the representation
\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\widehat{\mathsf{SL}}(3,\mathbb{R})
-factors through an
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin representation. As
\beta
is assumed to be nonzero,
\hat{\rho}
is not Fuchsian.

When
\omega\neq 0,
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-representation
\rho_{\omega}
is nontrivial. However, the pullback of the Higgs bundle to the connected double cover
X_{\omega}\to X
defines a point in
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{0}(X_{\omega})
.
Hence the restriction of
\hat{\rho}
to the kernel of
\rho_{\omega}
is an
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin representation which is not Fuchsian.

Conversely, let
[\rho,f]
be an equivariant holomorphic curve such that
f(\widetilde{\Sigma})
lies in a totally geodesic subspace
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}\subset\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Let
\widetilde{W}
be the normal bundle of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. If we denote by
\widetilde{M}
the orthogonal of
\mathrm{T}\widetilde{\Sigma}\oplus\mathrm{N}\widetilde{\Sigma}
inside
f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
, then
f^{*}\widetilde{W}
and
\widetilde{M}
descends respectively to real line bundles
W
and
M
on
\Sigma
. Thus we have that
\mathrm{B}\Sigma=W\oplus M
is topologically trivial, and so has degree
0
. Thus,
[\rho,f]
defines a point in
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
∎

5.6.
Link with affine spheres

The theory of Hitchin representations in
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
is intimately linked with the theory of
affine spheres
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Namely, Loftin
[
23
]
and Labourie
[
20
]
proved that given a Hitchin representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
, there exists a unique
\rho
-equivariant
affine sphere

u_{\rho}:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Such an affine sphere defines a metric
g
on
\Sigma
(called the
Blaschke metric
) and a holomorphic cubic differential
q_{3}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{K}^{3})
, called the
Pick form
, where
X
is the Riemann surface associated to
g
. This defines a map from
\mathrm{Hit}(\Sigma,\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R}))
to
\mathcal{Q}_{3}^{0}\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
which they proved is a homeomorphism.

Theorem
5.14
implies that the component
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{0}(\Sigma)
consists of equivariant holomorphic curves
[\rho,f]
lying in a totally geodesic copy of
\mathbf{H}^{3,2}
in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and such that the underlying representation comes from a non-Fuchsian Hitchin representation
\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
. The next theorem describes a link between the holomorphic curve
f
and the affine sphere
u_{\hat{\rho}}
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
(\rho,f)
be an alternating equivariant holomorphic curve whose equivalence class lies in
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{0}
,
z
be a point in
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
fixed by
\rho
,
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{+}
be isotropic
3
-plane defined in Subsection
3.3
and denote by
\hat{\rho}
the corresponding Hitchin representation into
\text{Fix}_{\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}}(x)\cong\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
. Then the map

\begin{array}[]{llll}u_{z}:&\widetilde{\Sigma}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{D}^{+}_{z}\\
&x&\longmapsto&f(x)+z\cdotp f(x)\end{array}

is a
\hat{\rho}
-equivariant affine sphere.

Proof.

Since
f
takes value in
z^{\bot}
, one easily checks that
u_{z}
as well. Moreover, we have

\psi_{z}(u_{z}(x))=z\cdotp u_{z}(x)=z\cdotp f(x)+z^{2}\cdotp f(x)=u_{z}(x)~,

so
u_{z}
takes value in
\mathsf{Ker}(\mathrm{Id}-\psi_{z})=\mathcal{D}^{+}_{z}
.

To see that
u_{z}
is an affine sphere, observe that
u_{z}
is obtained by considering the section
1
of
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{F}_{+}
which corresponds to an affine sphere by Baraglia
[
4
, Section 3.4.2]

∎

Remark 5.17
.

Note that the representation
\rho
fixes two points
z
and
-z
in
\mathbf{S}^{3,3}
. Taking
-z
instead of
z
is equivalent to considering the map
(f-z\cdotp f)
which takes value in
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{-}
. The underlying representation is the contragradient of
\hat{\rho}
.

5.7.
Totally geodesic surfaces

In the correspondence between alternating holomorphic curves and
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic bundles of the form of equation (
25
), the section
\beta
is the
(1,0)
-part of the second fundamental form of the holomorphic curve. As alternating holomorphic curves are assumed to be not totally geodesic,
\beta
is nonzero.

In the equivariant setting, stability of the Higgs bundle implies the section
\delta
is not
0
when
0\leq\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})\leq g-1
, see Proposition
5.6
. On the other hand, setting
\beta=0
gives rise to polystable Higgs bundles if and only if
0\leq\text{deg}(\mathcal{B})\leq g-1
. Such Higgs bundles are given by

They correspond to equivariant holomorphic curves
(\rho,f)
with zero second fundamental form, and hence the image of
f
is a totally geodesic holomorphic disk in
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
. Such a holomorphic disk is the intersection of
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
with a copy of
\Im(\mathbb{H}^{\prime})
, where
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
is a split-quaternion subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
. In particular,
\rho
factors through the stabilizer of
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
which is isomorphic to
\mathsf{SO}(2,2)
by Lemma
2.5
.

The
\mathsf{SO}(2,2)
-representation obtained can be described explicitly.
Choose a square root
\mathcal{K}^{\frac{1}{2}}
of the canonical bundle and let
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
(\mathcal{F}_{2},\varphi_{2})
be the following
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
Higgs bundles

(\mathcal{F}_{1},\varphi_{1}):=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (\mathcal{E}_{1},\Phi_{1})
is the Higgs bundle corresponding to the Fuchsian representation
\rho_{1}
uniformizing
X
, while the second one corresponds to a representation
\rho_{2}:\pi_{1}\Sigma\to\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with Euler class
g-1-d
. Moreover, we have

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.54167pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.54167pt\raise meaning that, under the

.

.

In particular,
[\mathcal{E},t\Phi]
corresponds to an equivariant alternating holomorphic curve
[\rho_{t},f_{t}]\in\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
for
0\leq d\leq g-1
. Taking the limit as
t
goes to
0
, we get that
[\rho_{t},f_{t}]
converges to an equivariant totally geodesic holomorphic curve.

Recall from Theorem
5.4
that for
0\leq d\leq g-1
the spaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}(X)
are biholomorphic to the quotient of the complement of the zero section of a rank
5g-5-d
vector bundle by the action of
\pm\mathrm{Id}.
By the above discussion, we see that the totally geodesic holomorphic curves correspond to the zero section of this vector bundle.
In particular,

(28)

\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{d}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)~,

where the fiber over
X\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{d}(\Sigma)
is the quotient by
\pm\mathrm{Id}
of the rank
5g-5-d
vector bundle. By construction
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{d}(\Sigma)\setminus\mathcal{H}_{d}(\Sigma)
is the zero section of this vector bundle and corresponds to totally geodesic equivariant holomorphic curves.

6.
Cyclic surfaces and infinitesimal rigidity

In this section, we introduce the notion of
cyclic surfaces
(Definition
6.4
), which is slightly more general than the one introduced by Labourie in
[
19
]
. Cyclic surfaces are a special type of holomorphic curves in a homogeneous space
\mathbf{X}
which we call the
cyclic space
(Definition
6.2
). The cyclic space in particular fibers over both over
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
and the Riemannian symmetric space
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
.
An important property of cyclic surfaces is that their projection to
\mathbf{H}^{4,2}
are (possibly branched) alternating holomorphic curves and their projection to
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
are (possibly branched) minimal surfaces whose underlying harmonic bundle is cyclic.

To ensure non-branched immersions in both case, we restrict to the class of
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces. We prove that
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces arise from the Frenet lift of a unique alternating holomorphic curve in Theorem
6.7
. We then use this interpretation to prove that equivariant alternating holomorphic curves
[\rho,f]
are infinitesimally rigid in Theorem
6.8
.

6.1.
Lie theory

Fix a maximal torus
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
in
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and denote by
\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}}
its Lie algebra. Let
\Delta\subset(\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}})^{*}
be the set of roots and, for any
\gamma\in\Delta
, denote by
\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
the corresponding root space. The root space decomposition is then

\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}~.

Writing
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}}
, we have that
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma+\delta}
for any
\gamma
and
\delta
in
\Delta\cup\{0\}
. In particular, if
\gamma+\delta
is not in
\Delta\cup\{0\}
we have
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},\mathfrak{g}_{\delta}]=0
.

Choosing a subset
\Delta^{+}\subset\Delta
of positive roots defines two simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
where
\alpha_{1}
is the short root. Explicitly, we have

\Delta^{+}=\big\{\alpha_{1}~,~\alpha_{2}~,~\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}~,~2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}~,~3\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}~,~3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}\big\}~.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

Any root
\gamma\in\Delta^{+}
is uniquely written as
\gamma=n\alpha_{1}+m\alpha_{2}
with
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
; we define the
length
of
\gamma
as

\ell(\gamma):=n+m~.

The longest root is
\eta:=3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
which has length
5
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}
set

\mathfrak{g}_{k}=\begin{cases}\bigoplus\limits_{-\ell(\gamma)=k}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}&\text{ if }k\neq 0\\
\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}}&\text{ if }k=0\end{cases}~.

The
cyclic grading
is then defined to be the
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-grading

(29)

\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}~.

In particular, we have
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{1}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{2}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a Cartan involution
\theta
on
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\rho(\mathfrak{g}_{k})=\mathfrak{g}_{-k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}
.

Proof.

This can be proved by choosing a Chevalley basis (see
[
19
]
for more details).
∎

Fixing such a Cartan involution, we denote by
\mathsf{K}
the associated maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and by
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathsf{K}
the maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular,
\mathsf{K}
is the compact real form of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}):=\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}/\mathsf{K}
is the Riemannian symmetric space of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Denote the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{T}
by
\mathfrak{t}
. The cyclic grading (
29
) gives an
\text{Ad}(\mathrm{T})
-invariant splitting

(30)

\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}~.

We will denote by
\mathfrak{t}^{\bot}
the direct sum
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
.

Define the
\mathbb{C}
-linear involution
\sigma
of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
by

\sigma|_{\mathfrak{g}_{k}}=(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{k}}~,~k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}~.

Since the grading is even, one easily checks that
\sigma
is an automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover,
\sigma
commutes with
\theta
and the corresponding real form
\lambda:=\sigma\circ\theta
has fixed point the Lie algebra of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
.

One can be more explicit in the description of the objects above. Consider the basis
(f_{-3},f_{-2},...,f_{3})
of
\Im(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{C}
as in equation (
7
), and let
F_{k}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{f_{k}\}
for each
k
. The complex quadratic form
\mathbf{q}
identifies
F^{*}_{a}
with
F_{-a}
for any
a
while the map
(x,y)\mapsto\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2}}x\times y
identifies
F_{-2}
with
F_{-3}\otimes F_{1}
.
The subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
preserving the splitting
F_{-3}\oplus F_{-2}\oplus\cdots\oplus F_{3}
is a maximal torus
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular, the vector space
\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
can be seen as the tangent space to
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}/\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
at the point
e\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
and thus embeds in
\bigoplus_{k\neq l}\operatorname{Hom}(F_{k},F_{l})
.
Furthermore, any root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
embeds in
\bigoplus_{k=-3}^{3-l}\operatorname{Hom}(L_{k},L_{k+l})
where
l=\ell(\gamma)
. We can thus represents any element
(a,b,d)\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{1}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{2}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}
as

(31)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.50002pt\raise 55.49963pt\raise 4.44806pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
