where, as in (
25
), the map
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}a:F_{-3}\to F_{-2}
is defined by
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}a(f_{-3})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}a(f_{0})\times f_{-3}
.

6.2.
Cyclic surfaces

We continue to use the notation from the previous section.

Definition 6.2
.

The
cyclic space

\mathbf{X}
is the homogeneous space
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}/\mathsf{T}
.

The natural projection
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{X}
is a principal
\mathsf{T}
-bundle. For any linear action of
\mathsf{T}
on a vector space
V
, we denote by
[V]=\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\underset{\mathsf{T}}{\times}V
the associated vector bundle over
\mathbf{X}
.

Let
D\in\Omega^{1}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
be the Maurer-Cartan form on
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. The Maurer-Cartan equation is then

(32)

dD+\frac{1}{2}[D\wedge D]=0~.

Using the
\text{Ad}(\mathrm{T})
-invariant splitting of equation (
30
), we can postcompose
D
with the projection on each factor. The projection onto
\mathfrak{t}
gives a connection
A
on the principal
\mathsf{T}
-bundle
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. The 1-form
\omega:=D-A
vanishes on the vertical tangent space of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{X}
and thus descends to a nowhere vanishing
1
-form on
\mathbf{X}
with values in the associated bundle
[\mathfrak{t}^{\bot}]
. In particular, the
\omega
identifies
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
with
[\mathfrak{t}^{\bot}]
.
Since the splitting
\mathfrak{t}^{\bot}=\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
is
\mathsf{T}
-invariant, we obtain

(33)

\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}=[\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}]\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}}[\mathfrak{g}_{k}]~.

The involutions
\theta,\sigma
and
\lambda
of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
extends equivariantly to involutions on
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
which we denote with the same symbols. Since
\lambda
is an automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the distribution in
\mathbf{X}
given by the fixed points of
\lambda
is integrable and its leaves correspond to orbits of subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
conjugated to
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}
we denote by
\omega_{k}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbf{X},[\mathfrak{g}_{k}])
the projection of
\omega
on
[\mathfrak{g}_{k}]
. Similarly, for any root
\gamma
, we denote by
\omega_{\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}}
and
\omega_{\gamma}
the projection of
\omega
on
[\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}]
and
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}]
respectively. Projecting Maurer-Cartan equation on
[\mathfrak{t}]
and
[\mathfrak{g}_{k}]
for
k\neq 0
, we get

(34)

\textstyle{F_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i+j=0}[\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}]^{\mathfrak{t}}=0}
\textstyle{d^{A}\omega_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i+j=k}[\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}]=0}

where
[.,.]^{\mathfrak{t}}
denotes the projection of the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{t}
parallel to
\mathfrak{t}^{\bot}
.

Definition 6.3
.

The
cyclic distribution

\mathcal{D}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
is the intersection of the fixed points of
\lambda
with
[\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1}]
.

The
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant endomorphism

\mathcal{J}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.72227pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.72227pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.21068pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 42.21068pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{1}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-14.59953pt\raise-7.16666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(x,y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.59955pt\raise-7.16666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.72227pt\raise-7.16666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.72227pt\raise-7.16666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-\mathrm{i}x,\mathrm{i}y)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

commutes with
\lambda
and square to
-\mathrm{Id}
. Thus, it induces an almost complex structure
\mathcal{J}
on
\mathcal{D}
.

Given a smooth oriented surface
S
, an orientation preserving smooth map
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
will called a
holomorphic curve in

\mathbf{X}
if
f
is tangent to the cyclic distribution and
df(\mathrm{T}S)
is
\mathcal{J}
-invariant.
Given
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0\}
, we denote by
df_{k}
the 1-form
\omega_{k}(df)
with value in
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{k}]
. Similarly, for any root
\gamma\in\Delta
, we denote by
df_{\gamma}
the form
\omega_{\gamma}(df)
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
be a holomorphic curve,

•

f
is a cyclic surface if
df_{-\alpha_{1}}
and
df_{-\alpha_{2}}
are not identically zero,

•

f
is an
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surface if it is a cyclic surface and
df_{-\alpha_{1}}
is nowhere vanishing.

Remark 6.5
.

In
[
19
]
, a cyclic surface is assumed to have
df_{-\alpha_{i}}
nowhere vanishing for all simple roots
\alpha_{i}.
When considering cyclic surfaces which are equivariant under surface group representations, this assumption forces the representation to be in the Hitchin component. Insisting
df_{\alpha_{i}}
is nowhere vanishing for only one simple root allows more flexibility, and is still strong enough to ensure the infinitesimal rigidity of Theorem
6.8
. This loosening of the definition also appears in
[
6
]
. In
[
8
]
, we consider a more general setup which guarantees infinitesimal rigidity.

6.3.
Cyclic surfaces and cyclic harmonic bundles

We now describe how cyclic surfaces are a differential geometric interpretation of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundles.
Given a cyclic surface
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
, denote by
\nabla=f^{*}A
the pull-back connection on
f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
be a cyclic surface, then

(1)

There is a unique complex structure
\mathsf{j}
on
S
such that, for any root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}
in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, the form
df_{\gamma}
has type
(1,0)
. In particular
\phi:=df_{1}
is of type
(1,0)
.

(2)

Up to postcomposing
f
with an element in
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the map
f
takes value in a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-orbit.

(3)

The Maurer-Cartan equations are equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}F_{\nabla}-[\phi\wedge\rho(\phi)]&=&0\\
\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}\phi=0&&\end{array}\right.,

where
\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}
is the
(0,1)
-part of
d^{\nabla}
.

Proof.

For the first item,
f
is an immersion away from a discrete set
D
. Thus, there exists a unique complex structure
\mathsf{j}^{\prime}
on
S\setminus D
such that
df\circ j=\mathcal{J}\circ df
. The complex structure
\mathsf{j}^{\prime}
extends uniquely on
S
. The result then follows from the fact that
\mathcal{J}
is the multiplication by
\mathrm{i}
on
[\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=[\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\beta}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}]
so the projection on each
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}]
is holomorphic.

The second item follows from the fact that
\mathcal{D}
is tangent to the distribution fixed by
\lambda
whose leaves are orbits of groups conjugated to
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
.

For the third item, note that the only nonzero terms in the pull-back of equations (
34
) are in degree
0,\pm 1,\pm 2
. Using
df_{-1}=-\rho(df_{1})
, we see that the equations in degree
-1
and
-2
are equivalent to the ones in degree
1
and
2
respectively. The equation in degree
0,1,2
are respectively

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 46.26385pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-46.26385pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{\nabla}-[\phi\wedge\rho(\phi)]=0~,}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 70.26385pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d_{\nabla}\phi=0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 136.94086pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{and}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 183.052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\phi\wedge\phi=0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces~.

Since
\phi
is a
(1,0)
-from,
\phi\wedge\phi
is automatically satisfied and
d_{\nabla}\phi=\bar{\partial}_{\nabla}\phi.

∎

Denote by
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
the (Riemannian) symmetric space of
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
, respectively. We see
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
as a totally geodesic subspace of
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
. Denote by
\pi:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
the natural projection. Note that a cyclic surface induces a Riemann surface structure
X=(S,\mathsf{j})
on
S.

Theorem 6.7
.

Given a cyclic surface
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
, the map
\pi\circ f:S\to\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
is a branched minimal immersion whose image is contained in
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
. Moreover, the corresponding
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-harmonic bundle on the induced Riemann surface has the form (
24
) with
\alpha
and
\beta
non-zero.

Conversely, given a
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-cyclic harmonic bundle on a Riemann surface
X
of the form (
24
) with
\alpha
and
\beta
non-zero, let
\rho
be the holonomy representation of the flat connection. Then the cyclic harmonic metric defines a conformal
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
h_{\rho}:\widetilde{X}\to\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
which lifts to a
\rho
-equivariant cyclic surface in
\mathbf{X}
.

Proof.

To prove that
\pi\circ f
is a branched minimal immersion, observe that, when
\mathbf{X}
is equipped with the pseudo-Riemannian metric induced by the Killing form, the map
\pi:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
is a pseudo-Riemannian submersion whose horizontal distribution is given by

\text{H}\mathbf{X}=\{x\in\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}~,~\theta(x)=-x\}~.

In particular, the horizontal distribution
\text{H}\mathbf{X}
is naturally identified with
\pi^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
. Moreover, this identification also identifies the restriction of
A
to
\text{H}\mathbf{X}
with the pull-back by
\pi
of the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
(this can be easily seen using the Kozsul formula).

Since the cyclic distribution
\mathcal{D}
is a subdistribution of
\text{H}\mathbf{X}
, the
(1,0)
-part of the differential of
\pi\circ f
is identified with
\phi=df_{1}
which is holomorphic by item
(3)
of Lemma
6.6
. It follows that
\pi\circ f
is harmonic.

To prove that
\pi\circ f
is a minimal immersion, we compute its Hopf differential. But since
f
is tangent to the horizontal distribution, the Hopf differential of
\pi\circ f
is equal to the one of
f
, which is
0
since
df_{1}
is tangent to the vector bundle
[\mathfrak{g}_{1}]
which is isotropic with respect to the Killing form. So
\pi\circ f
is weakly conformal and harmonic so is a branched minimal immersion.

The fact that
\pi\circ f
lies in
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
is a direct consequence of item (2) of Lemma
6.6
.

Consider now the associated
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
on
(S,\mathsf{j})
. In particular,
\mathcal{E}
is associated to the principal
\mathsf{K}^{\mathbb{C}}
obtained by complexifying the principal
\mathsf{K}
-bundle
(\pi\circ f)^{*}\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
(where we see
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
as a principal
\mathsf{K}
-bundle over
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
). The fact that
\pi\circ f
lifts to
\mathbf{X}
means that
\mathcal{E}
reduces to a holomorphic
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle which is compatible with the metric. Under this reduction, the Higgs field
\Phi=d(\pi\circ f)^{1,0}
lies in the bundle associated to the adjoint action of
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. According to (
31
), this exactly means that
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
has the form of (
24
) with
\alpha,\beta
and
\delta
corresponding respectively to
df_{-\alpha_{1}},df_{-\alpha_{2}}
and
df_{\eta}
.

Conversely, a diagonal harmonic metric on
(\mathcal{E},\Phi)
corresponds to a harmonic map to
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
which is compatible with the holomorphic
\mathsf{T}^{\mathbb{C}}
-structure on
\mathcal{E}
. This means that the harmonic map lifts to a map
f:(S,\mathsf{j})\to\mathbf{X}
. The Higgs field
\Phi
is identified with
df^{1,0}
which takes value in
[\mathfrak{g}_{1}]
.

By construction, the underlying
\mathsf{K}
-bundle reduces to a
\mathsf{T}
-bundle. This exactly means that
\mathcal{E}
splits holomorphically as a direct sum of line bundles. Similarly, the fact that the lift is cyclic means that
\Phi
as the form of (
24
), where
\alpha=df_{-\alpha_{1}}~,~\beta=df_{-\alpha_{2}}
and
\delta=df_{\eta}
.
∎

6.4.
Infinitesimal rigidity

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface. An equivariant cyclic surface is a pair
(\rho,f)
, where
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
is a representation and
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbf{X}
is a cyclic surface which is
\rho
-equivariant.

A
smooth family of equivariant cyclic surfaces
is a smooth map

F:\widetilde{\Sigma}\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\mathbf{X}

such that the map
f_{t}:=F(\cdot,t)
is a cyclic surface which is
\rho_{t}
-equivariant where the corresponding
[\rho]:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{X}(\Sigma,\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime})
is smooth. We denote by
(\rho_{t},f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
such a family,
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,0.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{f_{0}}=\left.dF(\partial_{t})\right|_{t=0}
the vector field along
f_{0}
and
[\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,0.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\rho_{0}}]=d_{0}[\rho](\partial_{t})
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
(\rho_{t},f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
be a smooth path of equivariant
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces such that
[\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,0.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\rho_{0}}]=0
. Then there exists a smooth path
(g_{t},\psi_{t})\in\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}\times\mathsf{Diff}_{0}(\Sigma)
such that
\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,0.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{f^{\prime}_{0}}=0
where
f^{\prime}_{t}=g_{t}\circ f_{t}\circ\psi_{t}
.

The proof follows (and simplify) the ideas introduced by Labourie in
[
19
]
.
A tangent vector
\zeta
to a smooth family
(f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of cyclic surfaces is called a
Jacobi field
. We see
\zeta
as a section of
f_{0}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
. Denote the projection of
\zeta
on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}]
by
\zeta_{-\alpha}
. The following proposition is the key technical result used to prove Theorem
6.8
. We postpone the proof until the final section.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbf{X}
be an
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surface and let
\zeta
be a Jacobi field along
f
. If
\zeta
is
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant and
\zeta_{-\alpha_{1}}=0
, then
\zeta=0
.

Proof of Theorem
6.8
using Proposition
6.9
.

Consider a smooth family
(\rho_{t},f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of equivariant
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces such that
[\overset{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,0.0)\circle*{2.0}\end{picture}\ }{\rho_{0}}]=0
. In particular, there exists a smooth path
(g_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
in
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}g_{t}\rho_{t}g_{t}^{-1}=0
. Hence, the tangent vector to the family
(g_{t}\circ f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
is a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant Jacobi field.

By assumption,
df_{-\alpha_{1}}
is never vanishing. So there exists a unique vector field
X
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
with
df_{-\alpha_{1}}(X)=-\zeta_{-\alpha_{1}}
. Let
\psi_{t}
be the flow of
X
at time
t
and define
f^{\prime}_{t}:=g_{t}\circ f_{t}\circ\psi_{t}
. In particular, each
f^{\prime}_{t}
is a
g_{t}\rho_{t}g_{t}^{-1}
-equivariant
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surface, and the tangent vector to the family
(f^{\prime}_{t})
is a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant Jacobi field
\zeta^{\prime}
which satisfies
\zeta^{\prime}_{-\alpha}=0
. The result follows from Proposition
6.9
.
∎

6.5.
Infinitesimal deformations of cyclic surfaces

The final section is devoted to the study of infinitesimal deformation of cyclic surfaces. In particular, we prove Proposition
6.9
.
We follow the idea of
[
19
]
, but our proof simplifies many relevant computations. We hope this sheds light on the important ingredients to ensure local rigidity.

Consider a Lie algebra bundle
\pi:\hat{E}\to M
on a manifold
M
, and let
\hat{\nabla}
be a compatible connection (that is
\hat{\nabla}
is a derivation for the Lie bracket). Consider
\Theta=\{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}\}
with
\theta_{k}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\hat{E})
. A
solution to the Pfaffian system
\Theta
is then a map
f:N\to M
such that
f^{*}\theta_{k}=0
for all
\theta_{k}\in\Theta
.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
be the differential ideal generated by
\Theta
.

Lemma 6.10
.

A map
f:N\to M
is a solution to the Pfaffian system
\Theta
if and only if
f^{*}\alpha=0
for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
.

Proof.

The direct implication follows from the fact that
f^{*}[\alpha\wedge\beta]=[f^{*}\alpha\wedge f^{*}\beta]
and
f^{*}d_{\hat{\nabla}}\alpha=d_{\nabla}f^{*}\alpha
for
\nabla=f^{*}\hat{\nabla}
. The converse follows from the fact that
\Theta\subset\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
.
∎

The following is proved in
[
19
, Proposition 7.14]
:

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
(f_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
be a smooth path of solution of the Pfaffian system
\Theta
and let
\zeta=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}f_{t}
. Then for any
\alpha=f^{*}\hat{\alpha}
with
\hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
we have

d^{\nabla}\circ\iota_{\zeta}\alpha=-\iota_{\zeta}\circ d^{\nabla}\alpha~.

Recall that the Maurer-Cartan equation implies that the connection
D=A+\omega
on
[\mathfrak{t}]\oplus\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
is flat, so
[\mathfrak{t}]\oplus\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
is isomorphic to the trivial Lie algebra bundle
\mathbf{X}\times\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Consider the Pfaffian system

\Theta:=\big\{\omega_{1}+\theta(\omega_{-1})~,~\omega_{\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}}~,~\omega_{j}\text{ for }j\in\mathbb{Z}_{6}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}\big\}~.

Lemma 6.12
.

The form
[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}]
belongs to the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
.

Proof.

The form
\omega_{-2}
is in
\Theta
, so
d^{A}\omega_{-2}
is in
\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
. But equation (
34
) for
k=2
gives

d^{A}\omega_{-2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i+j=-2}[\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}]=0~.

In
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
the equation
i+j=-2
implies
i=j=-1
or
i,j\notin\{\pm 1\}
. But for
i,j\notin\{\pm 1\}
we have
\omega_{i},\omega_{j}\in\Theta
so
[\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}]\in\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
. In particular, the above equation implies that
[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}]\in\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
.
∎

One easily checks that a map
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
is tangent to the cyclic distribution
\mathcal{D}
if and only if it is solution to the Pfaffian system
\Theta
.

Definition 6.13
.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
be a cyclic surface and
\nabla=f^{*}A
. A
Jacobi field
of
f
is a section
\zeta
of
f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
such that, for any
\alpha=f^{*}\hat{\alpha}
with
\hat{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}_{\Theta}
, we have

d^{\nabla}\circ\iota_{\zeta}\alpha=-\iota_{\zeta}\circ d^{\nabla}\alpha~.

Since cyclic surfaces are solution to the Pfaffian system
\Theta
, Proposition
6.11
implies that a vector field tangent to a deformation of cyclic surfaces is in particular a Jacobi field.

Let
f:S\to\mathbf{X}
be a cyclic surface with
\phi=df_{1}
and
\phi^{\dagger}=df_{-1}=-\theta(df_{1})
and let
\mathsf{j}
be the complex structure on
S
associated to
f
. Define

\textstyle{[\mathfrak{G}]=\bigoplus_{j\neq 0,\pm 1}[\mathfrak{g}_{j}]}
and
\textstyle{\Omega=\bigoplus_{j\neq 0,\pm 1}\omega_{j}.}

A Jacobi field
\zeta
which is tangent to a path of cyclic surfaces
f_{t}
with
f_{0}=f
, decomposes as

\zeta=\zeta_{0}+\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{-1}+Z,

where
\zeta_{j}=\iota_{\zeta}(f^{*}\omega_{j})
is a section of
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{j}]
and
Z=\iota_{\zeta}(f^{*}\Omega)
is a section of
f^{*}[\mathfrak{G}]
.

Proposition 6.14
.

A Jacobi field
\zeta
of a cyclic surface satisfies
\zeta_{0}=0
,
\lambda(Z)=Z
and
\zeta_{1}=-\theta(\zeta_{-1})
.

Proof.

The second item of Lemma
6.6
implies that, up to postcomposing
f_{t}
by an element in
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the cyclic surfaces
f_{t}
are contained in a given
\mathsf{G}_{2}^{\prime}
-orbit. Since the tangent space of such an orbit is
\text{Fix}(\lambda)
, we have that
\lambda(\zeta)=\zeta
.
But
\lambda(\zeta)=-\zeta_{0}-\theta(\zeta_{-1})-\theta(\zeta_{1})+\lambda(Z)
. Projecting on
[\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{t}]
gives
\zeta_{0}=0
, projecting on
\mathfrak{G}
gives
\lambda(Z)=Z
, while projecting on
[\mathfrak{g}_{1}]
gives
\zeta_{1}=-\theta(\zeta_{-1})
.
∎

The following appears in
[
19
, Proposition 7.6.1]
. For completeness, we repeat them here.

Lemma 6.15
.

(1)

d^{\nabla}Z=[(\zeta_{1}+Z)\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[(\zeta_{-1}+Z)\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}
.

(2)

[\partial_{\nabla}\zeta_{-1}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]=[[Z\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]

Proof.

For the first item, we have by Definition
6.13

d^{\nabla}Z=d^{\nabla}(\iota_{\zeta}(f^{*}\Omega))=-\iota_{\zeta}(d^{\nabla}(f^{*}\Omega))~.

However, projecting Maurer-Cartan equations on
[\mathfrak{G}]
gives

-d^{A}\Omega=\frac{1}{2}[\Omega\wedge\Omega]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[\omega_{0}\wedge\Omega]+[\omega_{0}\wedge\Omega]+\frac{1}{2}[\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{1}]+\frac{1}{2}[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}]+[\omega_{1}\wedge\Omega]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[\omega_{-1}\wedge\Omega]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.

Pulling-back by
f
, using
f^{*}\Omega=f^{*}\omega_{0}=0
and applying
\iota_{\zeta}
yields

-\iota_{\zeta}(d^{\nabla}(f^{*}\Omega))=[(\zeta_{1}+Z)\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[(\zeta_{-1}+Z)\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.

For the second item, first compute

(35)

d^{\nabla}(\iota_{\zeta}f^{*}[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}])=2d^{\nabla}[\zeta_{-1}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]=2[\partial_{\nabla}\zeta_{-1}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]~,

where we used the fact that
\phi^{\dagger}
has type
(0,1)
and satisfies
d^{\nabla}\phi^{\dagger}=0
. On the other hand, using Lemma
6.12
we have

(36)

d^{\nabla}(\iota_{\zeta}f^{*}[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}])=-\iota_{\zeta}\big(d^{\nabla}(f^{*}[\omega_{-1}\wedge\omega_{-1}])\big)=-2\big[\iota_{\zeta}(d^{\nabla}(f^{*}\omega_{-1}))\wedge\phi^{\dagger}\big]~.

But the Maurer-Cartan equations on
[\mathfrak{g}_{-1}]
gives

-d^{A}\omega_{-1}=[\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{-1}]+[\Omega\wedge\omega_{1}]^{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}+\frac{1}{2}[\Omega\wedge\Omega]^{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}~.

This yields
-\iota_{\zeta}(d^{\nabla}(f^{*}\omega_{-1}))=[Z\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}~.
Plugging back in equations (
36
) and comparing with equation (
35
) give the result.
∎

Proposition 6.16
.

A Jacobi field of a cyclic surface satisfies

\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z=\big[[Z\wedge\phi]\wedge\phi^{\dagger}\big]^{\mathfrak{G}}~,

were the superscript
\mathfrak{G}
denotes the projection on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{G}]
.

Proof.

Taking the
(0,1)
-part in the first item of Lemma
6.15
gives

\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z=[(\zeta_{-1}+Z)\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.
So,

\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z=[\partial_{\nabla}\zeta_{-1}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[\partial_{\nabla}Z\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.

By Lemma
6.15
we have

\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z=[[Z\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{g}_{-1}}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}+[[(\zeta_{1}+Z)\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{G}}\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.

Note that
[[\zeta_{1}\wedge\phi]\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]
is valued in
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{1}]
. So its projection on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{G}=]
vanishes. Moreover,
[Z\wedge\phi]
is valued in
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{-1}]\oplus f^{*}[\mathfrak{G}]
, so we obtain

\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z=[[Z\wedge\phi]\wedge\phi^{\dagger}]^{\mathfrak{G}}~.

∎

In the case
S=\widetilde{\Sigma}
and
\zeta
is
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant, the Bochner technique gives the following.

Proposition 6.17
.

Suppose
\zeta
is a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-invariant Jacobi field, then
Z=0
.

Proof.

Denote the Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
by
B
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle=B(\theta(\cdot,\cdot)
the corresponding Hermitian metric. By a slight abuse of notation, we denote in the same way their extension to the bundle of forms with value in
f^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathbf{X}
. On the one hand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\langle Z,\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}B(\theta(Z),d_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}B(d_{\nabla}\theta(Z),\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}B(\theta(d_{\nabla}Z),\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\langle\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z,\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0~,

where in the second line we used Stokes theorem, for the third we used the fact that
\nabla
preserves
\theta
and for the last one, we used the fact that in a local holomorphic coordinates
z
, the
(1,1)
-form
\langle\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z,\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z\rangle
is a nonnegative function times
dz\wedge d\overline{z}
.

On the other hand, by Propositions
6.14
and
6.16
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\langle Z,\partial_{\nabla}\overline{\partial}_{\nabla}Z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}B\left(\theta(Z),\big[[Z\wedge\phi]\wedge\phi^{\dagger}\big]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}B\left(\theta(Z)\wedge\phi^{\dagger},Z\wedge\phi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}B\left(\theta(Z\wedge\phi),Z\wedge\phi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\langle Z\wedge\phi,Z\wedge\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 0~,

where we used that, locally,
\langle Z\wedge\phi,Z\wedge\phi\rangle
is a nonnegative function time
d\overline{z}\wedge dz
.

This thus gives
Z\wedge\phi=0
. Applying the involution
\lambda
and using
\lambda(\phi)=-\theta(\phi)=\phi^{\dagger}
, we also obtain
Z\wedge\phi^{\dagger}=0
.
To show that
Z=0
, we compute the component
Z_{\gamma}
along
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}]
vanishes for each root
\gamma
. To do so, start by projecting the equation
[Z\wedge\phi]=0
on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{2}}]
. Using the root system of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain

[Z\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{2}}}=[Z_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\wedge df_{-\alpha_{1}}]=0~,

but since
df_{-\alpha_{1}}
is an isomorphism, this gives
Z_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}=0
. Similarly, we have

[Z\wedge\phi]^{\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}}=[Z_{2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\wedge df_{-\alpha_{1}}]=0~,

so
Z_{2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}
. The same argument gives
Z_{3\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}=0
. For the projection on negatives roots, we just use
\lambda(Z)=Z
and the fact that that
\lambda
maps
[\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}]
to
[\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}]
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.9
.

Proof of Proposition
6.9
.

Using the last two propositions, we get that a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-Jacobi field
\zeta
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
arising from a deformation of
\alpha_{1}
-cyclic surfaces has the form
\zeta=\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{-1}
. In particular, Proposition
6.16
gives

[\zeta_{1}\wedge\phi]=0~.

Assume
\zeta_{-\alpha_{1}}=0
, and project the above equation on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}]
. This gives

[\zeta_{-\alpha_{2}}\wedge df_{-\alpha_{1}}]=0~.

Again, the fact that
df_{-\alpha_{1}}
is an isomorphism gives
\zeta_{-\alpha_{2}}=0
.

Finally, the last component of
\zeta_{1}
is
\zeta_{\eta}=\zeta_{3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}}
. Projecting
[\zeta_{1}\wedge\phi]=0
on
f^{*}[\mathfrak{g}_{3\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}]
gives

[\zeta_{\eta}\wedge df_{-\alpha_{2}}]=0~.

Since
df_{-\alpha_{2}}
is holomorphic and not identically
0
, this implies that
\zeta_{\eta}=0
in the complement of a discrete set. By continuity,
\zeta_{1}=0
.
Since
\zeta_{-1}=-\theta(\zeta_{1})
, we conclude that
\zeta=0
.
∎
